# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Number of papers in this paper set: 258

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Bahbouhi, J. E., Bouderba, S. I., Elkouay, A., & Moussa, N. (2024). Effects of individual and collective decision rule on cooperation in public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: In a lab experiment comparing individuals and teams (majority and unanimity rule) in a repeated public goods game, teams with unanimity rule contributed more and achieved higher net profits than individuals in the absence of punishment. When punishment was introduced, all groups increased contributions, but teams (especially unanimity teams) used punishment less, particularly anti-social punishment. As a result, teams—especially unanimity teams—achieved higher net profits (efficiency) than individuals. The reduction in punishment was due to both a lower probability of punishing and a lower level of punishment when it occurred. The team decision process, especially under unanimity, filtered out extreme punishment preferences, reducing destructive punishment and increasing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard repeated public goods game with punishment, enabling punishment increases efficiency (net profits) for all groups, but the increase is larger for teams using unanimity rule than for individuals. The efficiency gain is due to both higher contributions and less costly punishment, especially less anti-social punishment. The game design dimensions—such as group size (individuals vs. 3-person teams), number of rounds, MPCR, punishment cost and technology, absence of chat, and feedback—are all clearly specified and match standard PGG designs. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, team decision rules (especially unanimity) are important moderators: unanimity teams achieve higher efficiency with punishment than individuals, mainly by reducing wasteful punishment. The control (no-punishment) efficiency is also higher for unanimity teams. Thus, for similar designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency, especially for teams with strong coordination requirements.

- source: Pi, J. X., Yang, G. H., & Yang, H. (2022). Evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in N-person snowdrift games with peer punishment and individual disguise. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a four-player public goods game with heterogeneous MPCRs (0.3 and 0.7), incomplete punishment networks (circle and homo-pairwise) lead to higher group contributions and higher efficiency (group payoff) than the complete punishment network. The complete punishment network, despite more potential punishers, results in lower contributions and lower efficiency, likely due to a bystander effect that reduces punishment severity. The group payoff (efficiency) is lowest in the complete punishment network (86.13 out of 160), and higher in the circle (94.81), homo-pairwise (95.39), and hetero-pairwise (92.01) networks. The structure of the punishment network thus significantly moderates the effect of punishment on efficiency. The study also finds that high-MPCR individuals contribute more than low-MPCR individuals, and that punishment severity is lower in the complete network. Antisocial punishment is present in all network types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence that the structure of the punishment network (i.e., who can punish whom) is a critical moderator of the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games with heterogeneous MPCRs. Specifically, enabling punishment by all (complete network) does not maximize efficiency; instead, certain incomplete networks (circle, homo-pairwise) yield higher efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the 'punishment_tech' and the network structure (not just the presence of punishment) must be considered: more potential punishers do not always increase efficiency, and may even reduce it. The control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the relative efficiency across punishment network types with punishment enabled is clear. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency as a function of punishment network structure and game design dimensions.

- source: Cobo-Reyes, R., Katz, G., Markussen, T., & Meraglia, S. (2022). Voting on sanctioning institutions in open and closed communities: experimental evidence. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares public goods games with endogenous institutional choice (formal vs. informal sanctions) in closed (fixed group) and open (migration allowed) societies. Formal sanctions always yield higher average payoffs and contributions than informal sanctions, but the gap is much larger in open societies. In closed societies, informal sanctions can sustain high cooperation and efficiency if initial group cooperation is high, but this is undermined when migration is allowed. The popularity and effectiveness of informal sanctions are fragile to group openness. The main efficiency outcome is average individual payoff per period, which is significantly higher under formal sanctions than informal sanctions in open societies (MT: FS=65.59, IS=60.63), but not significantly different in closed societies (NMT: FS=68.29, IS=67.59).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment (specifically, formal/centralized punishment) is larger in open societies (where group membership is endogenous) than in closed societies. When predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, the presence of migration or endogenous group formation is a key moderator: punishment is more effective at raising efficiency when groups are open. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR), punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness are all specified, allowing for precise mapping to prediction dimensions. Control (no-punishment) efficiency is not directly reported, but the comparison between informal and formal sanctions provides a strong basis for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. The results are most informative for settings with 5 players, 30 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and the specific punishment/reward technologies described.

- source: Sun, X. P., Liu, X. Z., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). The impact of reputation-driven locally variable synergy factors on public goods games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing manipulators—who both punish defectors and reward cooperators—into a spatial public goods game can sustain cooperation and increase average group payoff, even when the synergy factor (r) is low and cooperation would otherwise fail. The model shows that for a range of parameter values (punishment/reward efficiency, manipulation cost), manipulators and cooperators can coexist, and the group achieves higher payoffs than in the absence of punishment/reward. When punishment is sufficiently efficient (beta/gamma high), the system can reach the all-cooperator state, maximizing group payoff. If the cost of manipulation is too high or punishment/reward is inefficient, cooperation collapses and payoffs drop. Reward withdrawal (manipulators reducing rewards when surrounded by cooperators) reduces the average payoff of cooperators and narrows the payoff gap between manipulators and cooperators. The model demonstrates that the combined effect of punishment and reward is robust but sensitive to parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-signal evidence that enabling punishment (and reward) in a spatial public goods game increases group efficiency and average payoff, especially when the control (no punishment/reward) condition would result in low cooperation and low payoff. The effect is moderated by the efficiency of punishment (punishment impact per cost), the cost to punishers/rewarders, and the synergy factor (mpcr equivalent). The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, enabling punishment (especially when combined with reward) will generally increase efficiency, provided the punishment is not too costly and is sufficiently effective. The model also highlights that the presence of defectors and the structure of the population (spatial lattice) are important, but the main prediction is robust across a range of parameter values. This paper should be weighted heavily when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar spatial PGG designs.

- source: Li, M. Y., Kang, H. W., Sun, X. P., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2022). Replicator dynamics of public goods game with tax-based punishment. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that tax-based punishment can sustain cooperation and increase group efficiency in a public goods game, provided the tax is sufficient to offset the cost of punishment. There is a critical threshold for the synergy factor (mpcr) and punishment fine, above which contributing strategies dominate and group efficiency is maximized. The model also shows that, while tax-based punishment and reward can achieve the same maximum cooperation level, the average payoff (efficiency) can be higher under tax-based punishment when punishment/reward parameters are modest relative to the returns from cooperation. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for when cooperation and high efficiency are stable, and compares the efficiency of tax-based punishment, tax-based reward, and tax-based pure punishment models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment (with tax support) on efficiency in public goods games. It shows that, for a given group size, number of rounds (infinite/replicator), mpcr, punishment cost, and tax, one can determine whether punishment will increase efficiency and by how much. The results are most applicable to infinite, well-mixed populations with compulsory tax and continuous contribution choices. The explicit conditions and payoff formulas can be used to predict treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design dimensions, especially for settings with tax-based punishment. The findings also clarify that, while cooperation rate and efficiency can be aligned, the average payoff (efficiency) is the key outcome, and it can be higher for tax-based punishment than for tax-based reward under certain parameter regimes.

- source: Sun, Z. B., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2023). State-Dependent Optimal Incentive Allocation Protocols for Cooperation in Public Goods Games on Regular Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Network Science and Engineering*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations (regular networks), the optimal allocation of a fixed institutional incentive budget between reward and punishment depends critically on the efficiency ratio of reward to punishment. When punishment is more efficient, pure punishment is optimal for maximizing cooperation and minimizing the payoff advantage of defectors. When reward is more efficient, pure reward is optimal. For intermediate efficiency ratios, an adaptive hybrid protocol—rewarding when cooperation is low, punishing when it is high—is optimal. These results are derived analytically and verified numerically. The findings differ from well-mixed populations, where the adaptive hybrid protocol is always optimal regardless of efficiency ratio. The main outcome is the expected payoff difference between defectors and cooperators, which is minimized under the optimal protocol, leading to higher group efficiency and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment (and reward) on efficiency in public goods games with institutional incentives on regular networks. The key moderator is the efficiency ratio of reward to punishment: if punishment is more efficient, enabling punishment (with a fixed incentive budget) increases efficiency and cooperation more than reward; if reward is more efficient, the reverse holds. For intermediate ratios, a state-dependent switch is optimal. The results are most applicable to games with institutional (not peer) incentives, fixed budgets, and structured (not well-mixed) populations. The findings can be used to predict the efficiency impact of enabling punishment (vs. reward or hybrid) given knowledge of the incentive budget, the efficiency of each incentive, and the network structure.

- source: Yang, Z. H., & Yang, Y. L. (2024). Evolutionary dynamics of tax-based strong altruistic reward and punishment in a public goods game. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game, both strong altruistic punishment and reward are unable to sustain cooperation when implemented individually and funded only by voluntary contributions, due to their high cost. However, when a tax is imposed on all participants and the proceeds are used to fund either punishment or reward institutions, cooperation and group efficiency are greatly enhanced. The model shows that with sufficient tax compensation, strong altruistic punishers or rewarders can coexist with cooperators, eliminating defectors and increasing group welfare. Furthermore, tax-based strong altruistic reward leads to higher levels of cooperation and efficiency than tax-based strong altruistic punishment under otherwise identical conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that institutional (tax-based) punishment and reward mechanisms can substantially increase group efficiency in public goods games, compared to traditional voluntary punishment/reward or no-sanction conditions. The results suggest that, for a given set of game design parameters (e.g., player count, MPCR, cost/effectiveness of punishment/reward), introducing a tax-funded punishment or reward institution will increase the average efficiency of the group, with reward institutions being even more effective than punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to settings where punishment/reward is implemented institutionally and funded by mandatory contributions (taxes), and less so to peer-to-peer or voluntary sanctioning. The results are based on evolutionary game theory and numerical simulations, not empirical experiments.

- source: Shen, Y., Yin, W. K., Kang, H. W., Zhang, H. G., & Wang, M. (2022). High-reputation individuals exert greater influence on cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper develops and analyzes a theoretical model of a public goods game with tax-based punishment and reward mechanisms. It shows that: (1) Tax-based rewards given directly to cooperators are more effective at promoting cooperation and group payoff than rewards given to punishers. (2) When rewards are used to support punishers, this can undermine the effectiveness of punishment and reduce overall cooperation and efficiency, as punishers and cooperators end up in competition. (3) The effect of punishment and reward on efficiency is highly sensitive to the way these mechanisms are implemented, with some parameter regimes leading to lower group payoff than the baseline. (4) Increasing taxes (used as rewards for cooperators) generally increases the proportion of cooperators and system efficiency, but excessive rewards to punishers can be detrimental.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games depends critically on the design of the reward and punishment mechanisms, especially whether rewards are given to cooperators or punishers. For prediction, it suggests that enabling punishment does not always increase efficiency: if rewards are used to support punishers, efficiency may decrease compared to control. If tax-based rewards are given to cooperators, efficiency is more likely to increase. The model also provides explicit payoff functions and equilibrium conditions that can be used to estimate efficiency under different parameterizations (e.g., player count, MPCR, punishment cost, reward structure). However, the results are theoretical and may not capture all behavioral nuances seen in experiments.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2025). The second-order probabilistic pool punishment proportional to the payoff difference can solve the punishment problem of previous studies. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the second-order probabilistic pool punishment proportional to payoff difference leads to higher average payoff than both standard pool punishment (which suffers from fixed costs) and peer punishment (which suffers from high per-punishment costs) in spatial public goods games. This mechanism is less effective at fully eliminating second-order free riders, but allows for coexistence of punishers and cooperators with high group efficiency. The results are robust across different network structures (regular and randomized ring lattices).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that a probabilistic, payoff-difference-based pool punishment mechanism can increase group efficiency relative to both standard pool and peer punishment, under the specified game parameters (n=5, r=3, c=1, punishment cost G=0.3, punishment impact B=1, peer punishment cost gamma=0.3, peer punishment impact beta=1). The findings suggest that, for spatial PGGs with these parameters, enabling this form of punishment will increase efficiency compared to no punishment or to standard punishment mechanisms. The results are most applicable to settings with continuous contribution, no communication, and fixed group size and structure. The study is simulation-based, so real-world generalizability may be limited, but the mechanism-level insight is strong for theoretical prediction.

- source: Cui, P. B., Wu, Z. X., & Zhou, T. (2022). Small world can alleviate the social dilemmas originating from self-regulation or community policing issues. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the introduction of prosocial punishment in public goods games, especially when implemented on small-world networks, can substantially increase group efficiency (average payoff) by suppressing defectors and supporting the dominance of cooperative strategies. The effect is strongest when the network structure balances local clustering (which supports network reciprocity) and shortcuts (which allow punishers to reach defectors). Both the strength of punishment (fine) and the sensitivity of punishment implementation are important moderators. Regular lattices support cooperation but mainly favor cooperator-driven punishers, while random networks suppress punishment and cooperation. Small-world networks enable both high cooperation and high efficiency, with an optimal range of network randomness maximizing the effect. The model provides direct predictions for how efficiency changes as a function of punishment parameters and network structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games increases efficiency, especially when the game is played on small-world networks with appropriate parameter settings (moderate randomness, sufficient punishment fine, and intermediate feedback sensitivity). The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency (without punishment), introducing punishment will lead to a substantial increase in efficiency if the network structure allows for both local clustering and shortcuts. The results also indicate that the effect of punishment is moderated by the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), punishment cost, and the network topology. The paper's detailed mapping of efficiency and strategy frequencies across parameter spaces can be used to inform quantitative predictions of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions.

- source: Lee, H. W., Cleveland, C., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Mercenary punishment in structured populations. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that mercenary punishment (punishers who do not contribute but punish defectors) can promote cooperation and increase group efficiency in structured populations, even without an additional tax, provided the punishment cost is not too high and the fine is sufficiently large. Introducing a tax to support punishers expands the parameter space where defectors are eliminated and efficiency is high, but only if the punishment cost is significant. If the fine is too low, the tax can actually harm cooperators and allow defectors to coexist with punishers, reducing efficiency. There is an optimal punishment cost that maximizes average payoff. The model also reveals regions of cyclic dominance among strategies, leading to complex spatial dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence on how punishment cost, punishment effectiveness (fine), and the presence of a tax to support punishment interact to determine group efficiency in a spatial public goods game. For prediction, it suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency relative to control (no punishment) if the punishment cost is moderate and the fine is high, especially if a tax is used to support punishers when costs are high. However, if punishment is too costly or the fine is too low, efficiency may not improve or may even decrease. The findings are most applicable to spatially structured games with three strategies (C, D, P), and the results are sensitive to the exact parameter values of punishment cost, fine, and tax. The paper does not provide empirical data but offers detailed phase diagrams and payoff analyses that can inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment under varying game design dimensions.

- source: Wang, Q. S., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics in state-feedback public goods games with peer punishment. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in public goods games with peer punishment, introducing state-based feedback (where the multiplication factor increases with the number of contributors) significantly increases the efficiency of the system by making full-cooperation equilibria more likely and more stable. Local state-feedback (where the multiplication factor depends on the fraction of contributors in the group) is especially effective, sometimes enabling the system to reach and maintain full cooperation even when the baseline multiplication factor is not high. The positive effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to changes in punishment cost and fine, and state-based feedback can partially mitigate the second-order free-rider problem. The main outcome is the proportion of the state space (attraction basin) leading to efficient, full-contribution equilibria, which increases with both punishment and state-based feedback.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in public goods games with peer punishment, enabling punishment increases efficiency (group payoff) compared to the control (no punishment), especially when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is enhanced by state-based feedback. The effect is robust to variations in punishment cost and fine, and is stronger when the feedback is local rather than global. For prediction, the presence of punishment, lower punishment cost, higher punishment effectiveness, and the presence of local state-feedback all predict higher efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Liu, J. Q., Zhang, Q. W., & Tang, R. (2024). Fostering cooperative evolution through probabilistic punishment and environmental feedback in public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a feedback-based probabilistic punishment mechanism in a public goods game can lead to three qualitatively different long-term outcomes: (1) complete defection with low punishment probability (low efficiency), (2) complete cooperation with high punishment probability (high efficiency), and (3) bistability, where either outcome is possible depending on initial conditions. The transition between these regimes is governed by key parameters such as the synergy factor (r), the unit cost of punishment (delta), and the feedback mechanism parameters. High synergy and low punishment cost favor efficient, cooperative equilibria, while low synergy and high punishment cost favor inefficient, defective equilibria. The model provides explicit payoff equations and stability analysis for each regime.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly sensitive to the synergy factor (mpcr), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the feedback structure linking cooperation and punishment probability. If the control game (no punishment) is inefficient (low cooperation), enabling punishment can, under favorable parameter settings (high mpcr, low punishment cost), shift the system to high efficiency. However, if punishment is too costly or the synergy is too low, punishment may not improve efficiency and may even be ineffective. The presence of bistability means that initial conditions (e.g., initial cooperation rate) can also determine the outcome. Thus, for prediction, the model suggests that the effect of punishment is not universally positive and depends on the interplay of these design dimensions.

- source: Lv, S. J., Li, J. Y., & Zhao, C. H. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in voluntary public goods game with shared-punishment. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in voluntary public goods games with shared-punishment, the introduction and increase of punishment fines (beta) promote cooperation and increase average payoffs, especially when the punishment cost (alpha) is not too high. In well-mixed populations, mild punishment is insufficient to sustain cooperation, but as the fine increases, cooperation becomes stable and can dominate, leading to higher efficiency. In structured populations, cooperation can emerge and coexist with defectors and loners under milder punishment than in well-mixed populations, but the threshold for full cooperation is higher. High punishment costs discourage participation and can lead to non-participation equilibria. The model provides phase diagrams and equilibrium analyses that directly relate game design parameters to efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games with voluntary participation and shared-punishment. It shows that higher punishment fines (beta) generally increase efficiency by promoting cooperation, but higher punishment costs (alpha) can reduce efficiency by discouraging participation. The model explicitly links player count (N), marginal per-capita return (r/N), punishment cost (alpha), and punishment effectiveness (beta) to equilibrium payoffs and efficiency. The results are relevant for both well-mixed and structured populations, with clear phase boundaries and thresholds. The findings can be used to inform predictions of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in settings with voluntary participation and institutional/shared punishment.

- source: Gao, S. P., & Li, N. (2023). Preference reversal and the evolution of cooperation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that consensual peer punishment in public goods games can increase cooperation and group efficiency, but only if the level of cooperators' preference for punishment is above a threshold and the consensus required to implement punishment is not too high. When preference reversal is introduced (i.e., cooperators may change their mind about punishing), the effect on efficiency and cooperation depends on the structure of reversal probabilities and thresholds. If those who initially do not want to punish are likely to reverse and join in punishment, cooperation and efficiency are promoted. If those who initially want to punish are likely to reverse and abstain, the effect is negative. When reversal probabilities are uniform, increasing reversal undermines the positive effect of punishment, and high reversal can eliminate cooperation and reduce efficiency. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is highly sensitive to the details of collective decision-making and preference instability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in public goods games depends on the interplay between group size, consensus thresholds, punishment cost and effectiveness, and especially the stability of individuals' preferences for punishment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling punishment will not always increase efficiency: the effect is positive only when the group is likely to reach consensus to punish and when cooperators' willingness to punish is stable or when preference reversal tends to increase the number of punishers. If preference reversal is common and leads to fewer punishers, punishment may not improve and can even reduce efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions (in terms of group size, punishment parameters, and preference dynamics) for when punishment will or will not increase efficiency, which can be mapped to the prediction dimensions.

- source: Ishikawa, B. Y. S., & Fontanari, J. F. (2025). Revisiting institutional punishment in the N-person prisoner's dilemma. *Theory in Biosciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment in public goods games can stabilize high-efficiency (all-punishers) equilibria, but only if the cost to punishers is sufficiently low relative to the fines imposed on non-punishers and defectors. Cost sharing among punishers (i.e., dividing the maintenance cost of the institution) expands the parameter region where high efficiency is possible and increases the basin of attraction for the all-punishers equilibrium. However, the all-defectors equilibrium (zero efficiency) is always stable, so initial conditions and parameter settings are crucial. The group size N and the minimum number of punishers required to establish the institution (K_m) both affect the threshold frequency of punishers needed for high efficiency. In finite populations, demographic noise can sometimes allow cooperators to fixate, but this does not generally lead to robust high-efficiency outcomes. The model does not include optional participation, peer punishment, or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for how institutional punishment, cost sharing, group size, and punishment cost parameters affect the efficiency of public goods games. For the prediction task, it shows that enabling institutional punishment can increase efficiency dramatically, but only if the punishment cost is not too high relative to the fines and the group can reach a critical mass of punishers. Cost sharing (lower per-punisher cost as more punishers join) makes high efficiency more likely. Larger groups require a higher absolute number of punishers, but the threshold frequency can vary non-monotonically with group size. If the control game (no punishment) is at low efficiency (all-defectors), enabling punishment can, under the right conditions, shift the system to high efficiency, but only if the cost/benefit ratios and initial conditions are favorable. The paper does not address peer punishment, rewards, or communication, so its findings are most applicable to institutional punishment in large, well-mixed groups.

- source: Wang, C. Q., & Huang, C. C. (2022). Between local and global strategy updating in public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Introducing the option for costly punishment in a repeated public goods game increases group contributions and group payoffs compared to the no-punishment baseline. This effect holds both when sanctions are observable (feedback given immediately) and when sanctions are unobservable (feedback delayed until the end). The mechanism is that subjects expect free riders to be punished and adjust their behavior to avoid being punished, even when they do not observe sanctions in real time. Sanctioning is mainly non-strategic and norm-driven, targeting free riders. The presence of punishment stabilizes cooperation and prevents the unraveling of contributions, with some groups achieving high cooperation and others not. The overall effect of punishment on efficiency (as measured by group payoffs and contributions as a fraction of maximum) is positive.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling costly punishment in a standard linear public goods game increases group efficiency (payoff) compared to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to whether punishment is observable or not during play, suggesting that the mere possibility of punishment, and the associated social norm, is sufficient to increase cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that adding punishment to a repeated linear PGG with 4 players, 10 rounds, and MPCR=0.4 will increase efficiency, even if punishment is not immediately observable. The magnitude of the effect may vary by group, but the direction is reliably positive. The paper also provides parameter values for key design dimensions.

- source: Wang, X. J., Yang, Z. P., Chen, G. C., & Liu, Y. L. (2024). Enhancing cooperative evolution in spatial public goods game by particle swarm optimization based on exploration and q-learning. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that a reputation-based, probabilistic punishment mechanism can robustly promote cooperation and increase group payoffs in spatial public goods games, provided that the co-beneficiary capacity (number of individuals who share in the redistributed benefits) is moderate and that participants' retaliatory sentiment and reputation memory are within optimal ranges. Stronger retaliatory sentiment (lower gamma) and higher synergy factors (r) expand the parameter space where cooperation and high payoffs are sustained. The mechanism is robust to initial conditions and can allow punishers to achieve high payoffs, mitigating the typical cost of punishment. However, if the co-beneficiary capacity is too small (punishment is too weak) or too large (second-order free-riding emerges), or if reputation memory is too lenient or too short, cooperation and group payoffs collapse.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in spatial public goods games, enabling a reputation-based probabilistic punishment mechanism can substantially increase group payoffs and cooperation rates, especially when the marginal per-capita return is high and the punishment/reward mechanism is tuned to avoid both excessive exclusivity and excessive inclusivity. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of such a punishment mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, provided the co-beneficiary capacity and reputation memory are in the optimal range. The results are most directly applicable to structured (networked) populations with local interactions and reputation tracking.

- source: Han, D., & He, Y. X. (2023). The impact of labor subsidy, taxation and corruption on individual behavior. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: Theoretical analysis and numerical simulations show that: (1) Taxation, when used to fund both labor subsidies for executors and the costs of reward/punishment, can promote and stabilize cooperation in public goods games. (2) Moderate labor subsidies for executors (who implement reward/punishment) are optimal for forming stable alliances of cooperators and executors, excluding defectors. (3) Excessive reward (relative to punishment) or excessive subsidies can destabilize cooperation and allow defectors to invade. (4) The presence of a corruption (bribery) mechanism has little effect on cooperation levels when subsidies are high, but can slow the increase of cooperation as taxation increases. (5) The main outcomes are the steady-state fractions of each strategy; average payoffs are derived but not the focus. (6) The model is entirely theoretical and does not provide empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical, mechanistic evidence about how the structure of punishment, reward, taxation, and executor subsidies affect the evolutionary stability of cooperation in public goods games. It is directly relevant for understanding how punishment and reward mechanisms (and their funding) interact to affect cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff as a fraction of the social optimum. The results suggest that moderate, well-funded punishment (and reward) mechanisms, especially when executors are compensated, can stabilize cooperation, but excessive reward or subsidy can backfire. The presence of corruption can reduce the effectiveness of punishment, but this effect is mitigated by higher subsidies. For prediction tasks, this paper informs the expected direction and possible moderators of punishment's effect on cooperation, but not directly on efficiency.

- source: Wang, S. X., Chen, X. J., Xiao, Z. L., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Decentralized incentives for general well-being in networked public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that both institutional punishment and reward, when applied optimally, can drive a population to full cooperation in a public goods game. However, the cumulative cost to the institution is lower for optimal punishment than for optimal reward across almost all initial conditions. The optimal control protocols for both incentives are derived analytically, and numerical examples confirm that these protocols minimize the cost of achieving cooperation compared to other fixed or heuristic strategies. The evolutionary trajectory of cooperation is identical under both optimal strategies, but punishment is more cost-efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in a standard public goods game with institutional incentives, enabling punishment (with optimally chosen intensity) is more cost-effective for achieving full cooperation than enabling reward, given the same game parameters. While the paper does not report group efficiency in the standard sense, the cumulative cost minimization is closely related to institutional efficiency. The results are most applicable to settings with well-mixed populations, no communication, continuous contribution, and institutional (not peer) punishment or reward. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, enabling institutional punishment (with optimal tuning) will yield higher efficiency (lower cost to reach cooperation) than enabling institutional reward, and both are superior to no incentive. However, the model does not address peer punishment, finite groups, or stochastic effects.

- source: Sun, X. P., Bi, Y. Z., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). Effect of distribution of fines on evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Redistributing fines from defectors to cooperators and punishers in a spatial public goods game generally increases the persistence of cooperation and raises the total system payoff, especially when punishment costs are high. When punishment costs are low, the standard pool punishment model (without redistribution) can achieve higher cooperation and higher total payoff, as punishers dominate and defectors are eliminated. The effect of fine redistribution is thus mixed: it is beneficial for efficiency and cooperation under high punishment cost, but can reduce efficiency and cooperation when punishment cost is low. The distribution parameter (alpha) also matters: allocating more fines to punishers (lower alpha) increases cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends on the cost of punishment and the mechanism for handling fines. If fines are redistributed to cooperators and punishers, efficiency losses from punishment can be mitigated, especially when punishment is costly. However, in settings where punishment is cheap, standard punishment (without redistribution) may yield higher efficiency by allowing punishers to eliminate defectors. For prediction, the presence or absence of fine redistribution, the punishment cost, and the fine distribution parameter (alpha) are important moderators. The results suggest that enabling fine redistribution is likely to increase efficiency when punishment is expensive, but may not always do so when punishment is cheap.

- source: Gao, S. P., Suo, J. H., & Li, N. (2025). Cooperation under endogenous punishment in the spatial public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that endogenous punishment can promote cooperation in spatial public goods games when the network reciprocity alone is insufficient (i.e., when the enforcement factor is below the threshold for cooperation in the absence of punishment). The effectiveness of punishment depends on the willingness of cooperators to punish (probability p), the cost and fine of punishment, and the network degree. There is a critical value of p above which cooperation can be sustained. However, when network reciprocity is already strong enough to support cooperation, adding endogenous punishment can actually hinder cooperation or have no additional effect. The evolutionary dynamics can be bi-stable, with both all-cooperator and all-defector states possible depending on initial conditions. The model provides explicit threshold formulas for when cooperation is favored.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how endogenous punishment, punishment cost and effectiveness, and network structure interact to affect the likelihood of achieving full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) in spatial public goods games. While it does not report empirical efficiency values, it gives explicit formulas for the thresholds at which cooperation becomes stable or dominant, as a function of the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and network degree. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment can increase efficiency (by supporting all-cooperation) when the control game is below the cooperation threshold, but may have little or even negative effect when the control game already achieves high cooperation due to network reciprocity. The model is most informative for structured populations with endogenous, probabilistic punishment.

- source: Lv, S. J., Zhang, X. X., Zhong, A. Y., Mi, J., & He, Q. L. (2022). Synergy and discount of punishment in the public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that nonlinear punishment (synergy or discount) significantly affects the evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in public goods games. In well-mixed populations, cooperation cannot be the unique stable equilibrium; both cooperation and defection can be stable depending on parameters and initial conditions. In structured populations (regular networks), cooperation can become the unique stable equilibrium under certain conditions. The effectiveness of punishment (synergy parameter), group size, network degree, and punishment severity all interact to determine whether cooperation is promoted. Synergistic punishment (increasing effectiveness with more punishers) can enlarge the domain of attraction for cooperation, especially in structured populations. The impact of network degree (group size) is non-monotonic and depends on the level of punishment severity. The paper provides explicit replicator equations and phase diagrams but does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how nonlinear punishment mechanisms, group size, network structure, and punishment severity interact to affect the evolutionary stability of cooperation in public goods games. While it does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it shows that synergistic punishment and certain network structures can make cooperation more stable, which would likely increase group efficiency in practice. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of synergistic punishment, lower punishment costs, and structured populations (as opposed to well-mixed) are likely to increase the efficiency gains from enabling punishment. However, the lack of explicit efficiency or payoff data limits direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Zhang, B. Y., & Pei, S. (2022). Game Theory and the Evolution of Cooperation. *Journal of the Operations Research Society of China*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews theoretical and empirical literature on the evolution of cooperation in public goods games and related social dilemmas. It finds that punishment (both peer and institutional) can promote cooperation, especially in one-shot or non-repeated interactions, but at a cost that can reduce group welfare or average payoff. Peer punishment can curb free-riding but is costly and can lead to second-order free-riding and antisocial punishment, often resulting in lower group payoffs compared to no-punishment conditions. Institutional punishment can overcome some of these issues but is also costly. Reward mechanisms are generally less effective than punishment in moving populations from defection to cooperation, unless the reward is large. The paper emphasizes that combining reward and punishment can have a strong effect on promoting cooperation. Theoretical models predict that the effect of punishment on cooperation and payoff depends on the size and structure of the incentive, and that punishment can eliminate extremely selfish behavior but does not always increase group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and review-based guidance that punishment mechanisms in public goods games can increase cooperation rates, but often at a cost that may reduce group efficiency or average payoff, especially in peer punishment settings. The effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and depends on the cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the structure of the game (e.g., peer vs institutional punishment, compulsory vs voluntary participation). For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation but may not always increase efficiency, and in some cases may reduce it due to the costs of punishment. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates or new empirical data, so its value is mainly in providing qualitative, mechanism-based expectations and summarizing prior findings.

- source: Shen, Y., Guo, J., & Kang, H. W. (2024). The Influence of Fine Distribution and Compensation on Cooperation in Public Goods Game. *Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that introducing probabilistic punishment into a spatial public goods game increases the prevalence of cooperation compared to the baseline. Adding fine distribution (punishers receive a share of fines from defectors) and compensation (punishers compensate cooperators when punishment fails) further increases the prevalence and stability of cooperation, especially across a broader range of punishment costs and intensities. The compensation mechanism is most effective at low punishment costs, while fine distribution is most effective at high punishment costs. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that probabilistic punishment, fine distribution, and compensation mechanisms can increase the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that, under similar game design dimensions (e.g., spatial structure, probabilistic punishment, fine distribution, compensation), enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, which may be associated with higher efficiency in many PGG contexts, but this is not directly demonstrated. The paper is most informative for predicting the direction and robustness of cooperation rate changes, not for quantitative efficiency predictions.

- source: Wang, Y., & Lu, S. N. (2025). Environmental-feedback-driven time-varying reputation update solve social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study demonstrates that in a continuous SPGG with punishment, the use of exploration-based particle swarm optimization (EPSO) and its parameter optimization via Q-learning (QPSO) can rapidly drive the system to a state of full cooperation (ACI=1) under certain parameter regimes. Both low and high values of the individual learning coefficient (c1), higher inertia weight (omega), lower punishment intensity (eta), and higher gain intensity (r) promote higher cooperation intensity. The main reported outcome is ACI, not direct group payoff or efficiency. The findings are based on simulation experiments with large populations (L=200, i.e., 40,000 agents) on a square lattice, and the results are robust across a range of parameter values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: while it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that punishment (with certain parameterizations) can lead to maximal cooperation intensity in a spatial PGG with continuous contributions. The results suggest that, in similar simulation/theoretical models, enabling punishment with low punishment intensity and high gain intensity can drive the system to near-full cooperation, which would likely correspond to high efficiency if mapped to payoff outcomes. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff reporting limits the direct applicability to the prediction task. The findings are most relevant for understanding how parameter settings (punishment intensity, gain intensity, learning coefficients) modulate the effect of punishment on cooperation, which may inform priors for efficiency outcomes in similar game designs.

- source: Flores, L. S., & Han, T. A. (2024). Evolution of commitment in the spatial public goods game through institutional incentives. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial PGG with pre-play commitment, both institutional punishment (of commitment non-compliance) and reward (of commitment compliance) can promote cooperation. Punishment is most effective at high commitment thresholds and with strong punishment, while reward can sustain cooperation at any threshold if the reward is strong enough. Intermediate thresholds require less severe punishment to sustain cooperation. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are limited to cooperation rates and strategy dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial PGGs with commitment, especially at high commitment thresholds and with strong punishment. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes. The results suggest that, in similar designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, which may correlate with higher efficiency, but this is not directly tested or quantified in terms of payoff.

- source: Feng, S. A., & Liu, X. S. (2023). Effects of the limited incentive pool on cooperation evolution in public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of a limited punishment pool in a spatial public goods game strongly suppresses defectors and promotes the dominance of cooperators, more so than a limited reward pool. Increasing the capacity or cost of the punishment pool further reduces defection and can lead to full cooperation under certain parameter ranges. In contrast, the reward pool is less effective, and its impact on defectors depends on the cost and capacity settings. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the results imply that punishment is a more robust mechanism for sustaining cooperation in resource-limited institutional settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial public goods games with limited institutional resources, punishment mechanisms are more effective than reward mechanisms at promoting cooperation and suppressing defection. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates and reduce defection, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly quantified. The model details (e.g., spatial structure, limited pool, agent types) should be considered when transferring these insights to other settings.

- source: Pancotto, F., Righi, S., & Takács, K. (2023). Voluntary play increases cooperation in the presence of punishment: a lab in the field experiment. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that punishment increases contributions in public goods games, both when participation is compulsory and when it is optional. The effect is strongest when participation is compulsory and punishment is available. Optional participation (opt-out) alone does not significantly increase contributions compared to compulsory participation. Loners (those who opt out) contribute less when forced to participate, but are not responsible for anti-social punishment. Instead, low contributors (free-riders) are the main source of anti-social punishment. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the contribution data suggest that punishment increases group welfare, especially in compulsory settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in discrete public goods games with 4 players, 1:3 punishment technology, and no communication, enabling punishment increases contributions, especially when participation is compulsory. While efficiency is not directly reported, the increase in contributions strongly suggests that efficiency would also increase with punishment, particularly in compulsory games. The design dimensions are well specified, allowing for mapping to prediction tasks, but the lack of direct efficiency or group payoff data means that predictions must be inferred from contribution outcomes. The results are most informative for settings with similar group size, discrete contributions, and punishment technology.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2025). Effects of punishment driven by inequity aversion on promoting cooperation in public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment driven by inequity aversion can promote the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when punishment costs are low and fines are high. Inequity-averse cooperators (IACs) can invade defectors and dominate the population under favorable parameter regimes, but their effectiveness is limited by internal conflict (punishing other cooperators or IACs) and the presence of non-punishing cooperators. Inequity-averse defectors (IADs) can also promote cooperation by forming protective barriers around cooperators, reducing the ability of defectors to invade. The effectiveness of punishment depends on the ratio of fine to cost, the synergy factor (MPCR), and the spatial structure. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms—specifically, those based on inequity aversion—can increase the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games, particularly when punishment is not too costly and fines are substantial. The findings suggest that the design of punishment (who is targeted, how costs and fines scale) and the spatial structure of interactions are important moderators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it supports the idea that punishment can change the behavioral composition of the group, which may be associated with higher efficiency in related models, but this link is not demonstrated here.

- source: Lv, S. J., & Song, F. F. (2022). Particle swarm intelligence and the evolution of cooperation in the spatial public goods game with punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of punishment on cooperation in a spatial public goods game depends on both the punishment intensity and the learning rule (PSO weighting coefficient). For low punishment intensity, low susceptibility to neighbors (low omega) promotes cooperation; for high punishment intensity, high omega is more beneficial. Intermediate omega values increase punishment input but can reduce cooperation. The enhancement factor (r) also moderates these effects. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are about cooperation rates and investment allocation, not direct welfare or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and learning rules affect cooperation rates and investment allocation in spatial PGGs with continuous strategies. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff with or without punishment. For the prediction task, the main value is in understanding that the effect of punishment on cooperation is highly sensitive to punishment intensity and the learning rule, and that the allocation of resources between cooperation and punishment can affect the expansion of cooperative clusters. However, since efficiency is not reported, the paper's findings should be used only as indirect, mechanistic context rather than as direct quantitative evidence for efficiency prediction.

- source: Qian, J., Sun, X., Wang, Z. Y., & Chai, Y. T. (2022). Negative Feedback Punishment Approach Helps Sanctioning Institutions Achieve Stable, Time-Saving and Low-Cost Performances. *Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The negative feedback punishment approach, in which punishment intensity decreases as group cooperation increases, leads to more stable and higher cooperation rates, requires less time to reach high cooperation, and results in lower cumulative punishment intensity (interpreted as lower cost) compared to constant or positive feedback punishment methods. Theoretical analysis shows that this approach allows for stable intermediate levels of cooperation, while constant or positive feedback methods tend to produce unstable or all-or-nothing outcomes. The findings are based on evolutionary simulations of a public goods game with pool punishment, but do not report efficiency or group payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the punishment rule (specifically, negative feedback between cooperation and punishment intensity) can substantially affect the stability and cost of achieving high cooperation in PGG-like environments. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that negative feedback punishment rules may reduce the cost (in terms of punishment applied) and time to reach high cooperation, but do not quantify the effect on group efficiency. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment rule design (punishment_tech) and dynamic adjustment can moderate the behavioral and cost outcomes of punishment institutions.

- source: Quan, J., Guo, H. T., & Wang, X. J. (2022). Impact of reputation-based switching strategy between punishment and social exclusion on the evolution of cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that conditional punishment based on reputation (switching between fines and exclusion) is more effective at promoting cooperation than traditional punishment alone. Higher reputation thresholds (stricter punishment), higher fines, and lower exclusion costs all lead to higher frequencies of cooperation. The enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) also strongly moderates the emergence and stability of cooperation. The model shows phase transitions between full defection and full cooperation, and regions where cooperation and defection coexist. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes (strategy frequencies).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms (including conditionality based on reputation, the severity of fines, and the cost of exclusion) can substantially affect the frequency of cooperation in spatial PGGs. While the findings are highly relevant for predicting the impact of punishment on cooperation rates, they are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency or group payoff, since these are not reported. The results suggest that, in similar spatial PGGs, enabling punishment (especially with flexible, reputation-based mechanisms) is likely to increase cooperation rates, which would generally be expected to improve efficiency, but the magnitude of efficiency gains cannot be directly inferred from this paper.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Quan, J., Ma, S. T., & Zhao, J. H. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in optional public goods games with loners' punishment. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of loners who punish cooperators (LP) into the optional public goods game leads to new evolutionary phases, including three-strategy cyclic states (C+D+LP), pure cooperator phases, and coexistence of cooperators with both types of loners. The presence and intensity of punishment (punishment cost and fine) modulate the transitions between these phases. Under certain conditions (e.g., high punishment fine and/or cost, high loner income), the system can escape the typical cyclic dominance and reach a pure cooperation phase or a coexistence phase where cooperators are protected by traditional loners from punishing loners. However, the paper does not quantify the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the prevalence and stability of cooperation as a strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how punishment by loners affects the prevalence of cooperation in optional public goods games, under varying game parameters (synergy factor, loner income, punishment cost, punishment fine). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for understanding how game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, loner income) affect the likelihood of cooperation dominating or persisting, which may be indirectly informative for efficiency predictions if one assumes that higher cooperation rates translate to higher efficiency. Caution is warranted, as the mapping from strategy frequencies to efficiency is not explicit in this work.

- source: Shi, Z. Y., Wei, W., Li, B. F., Li, C., Li, H. B., & Zheng, Z. M. (2022). Two-layer network model of public goods games with intervention and corruption. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment (umpire intervention) always increases the fraction of cooperators compared to no intervention, even if all umpires are corrupt and only accept bribes. The fraction of fair umpires, the spatial distribution of umpires, the punishment fine, and the bribe size all have strong effects on cooperation rates. In particular, higher bribe costs and higher punishment fines both increase cooperation, but the effect of bribe cost is more pronounced. The model is robust to network structure (square lattice vs. scale-free). However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment mechanism (even if imperfect or corrupt) increases cooperation rates in PGG-like environments. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcome, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates as a function of punishment design, but not for predicting group welfare or surplus. The model's detailed exploration of punishment cost, effectiveness, and corruption is informative for mechanism design, but the lack of payoff reporting is a key limitation for efficiency prediction.

- source: Kang, H. W., Xu, Y. H., Chen, Q. Y., Li, Z. K., Shen, Y., & Sun, X. P. (2024). The role of reputation to reduce punishment costs in spatial public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a reputation-based punishment mechanism in a spatial public goods game model allows punishers to reduce their punishment costs as their reputation increases. Simulation results show that this mechanism enables the system to achieve global cooperation and expel betrayers even at lower synergy factors, where traditional punishment fails. The model demonstrates that clusters of punishers with high reputation can resist and eliminate betrayers more effectively, and that parameter ranges (synergy factor, punishment cost, fine) determine the system's ability to reach cooperation. However, the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes; instead, it focuses on the prevalence of strategies and the system's convergence to cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that reducing punishment costs via a reputation mechanism can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when the marginal per-capita return (synergy factor) is low. For the downstream prediction task, the findings suggest that punishment mechanisms with lower or dynamically reduced costs (e.g., via reputation) are likely to yield higher efficiency than traditional, high-cost punishment, particularly in low-MPCR environments. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The results are most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of punishment cost and reputation mechanisms on cooperation dynamics.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Quan, J., Ma, S. T., & Zhao, J. H. (2025). The evolution of cooperation and punishment in spatial public goods games with bribery. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games with peer punishment, the introduction of bribery (defectors bribing punishers to avoid sanctions, and punishers accepting bribes) leads to complex phase transitions among strategies. When punishment intensity is low, bribery can increase the fraction of contributors (interpreted as higher cooperation), as bribe-recipients and bribers form alliances that resist defectors. As the bribe amount increases, the system can enter cyclic coexistence states involving bribers, bribe-recipients, and defectors. When punishment intensity is high, bribery undermines the maintenance of punishment and can reduce the contribution level. The effect of bribery is highly sensitive to the synergy coefficient (analogous to MPCR), punishment fine, and bribe amount. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the prevalence of strategies and contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and bribery mechanisms interact to affect cooperation in spatial PGGs, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by proxy, efficiency) is moderated by the possibility of bribery and the parameters of the game (synergy coefficient, punishment fine, bribe amount). In environments where punishment is weak, bribery can increase cooperation; where punishment is strong, bribery can undermine it. However, since efficiency is not directly measured, these results should be used cautiously for predicting efficiency outcomes.
