- source: Peng, H. C. (2022). Effects of majority-vote reward mechanism on cooperation: a public good experimental study. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The majority-vote reward mechanism significantly increases group efficiency and cooperation compared to both peer reward and no reward, in both homogeneous and heterogeneous MPCR settings. Peer reward increases efficiency and cooperation only in homogeneous groups. In heterogeneous groups, only the majority-vote reward mechanism leads to a significant increase in efficiency. The efficiency gains are due to higher contributions and more willingness to pay reward costs under the majority-vote mechanism. The paper provides direct evidence on efficiency outcomes (Table 6), showing that the majority-vote reward mechanism achieves the highest efficiency among the tested mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with 4 players, 6 rounds per condition, continuous contribution, no chat, and full information, adding a majority-vote reward mechanism (with a 1:3 cost-to-reward ratio) increases efficiency relative to the control (no reward) and peer reward conditions. Peer reward also increases efficiency in homogeneous groups, but not in heterogeneous groups. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with similar design dimensions, especially regarding the presence and type of reward mechanism, MPCR homogeneity, and the absence of punishment. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, majority-vote reward is a robust way to increase efficiency, especially when group members have heterogeneous returns from the public good.

- source: Yang, Q. X., & Yang, Y. L. (2024). A social monitoring mechanism for third-party judges promotes cooperation in evolutionary games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in repeated PGGs without punishment or reward, cooperation and group payoff decline over time, consistent with previous literature. The authors reconstruct a micro-dynamic model based on proportional changes in expected payoff, showing that players' willingness to cooperate depends on their current strategy and local cooperation levels. The model predicts the observed decline in cooperation and group payoff. The paper also documents a 'restart effect' in two-stage games, where cooperation temporarily increases at the transition point. The findings provide a framework for predicting the evolution of cooperation and group payoff in repeated PGGs without external interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on baseline efficiency and group payoff dynamics in repeated PGGs without punishment or reward. It is directly relevant for predicting control (no-punishment) efficiency as a function of game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, MPCR, and information structure. However, it does not provide any evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment-related variables. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, but it is highly informative for modeling the baseline (control) case.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Coevolutionary dynamics of population and institutional rewards in public goods games. *Expert Systems with Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with institutional rewards, if the reward strength is adaptively increased when cooperation is low and decreased when cooperation is high, the system can reach stable equilibria with sustained cooperation and group welfare. The model shows that: (1) a stable coexistence of cooperation and reward strength is possible; (2) full cooperation can be achieved with minimal reward intensity if the per capita incentive is above a threshold; (3) moderate cooperation can be sustained with either low or high reward intensity, depending on parameter values; (4) in some cases, even high reward intensity cannot prevent full defection if incentives are too low. The model provides explicit conditions for these outcomes in terms of group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with institutional rewards, but not for games with punishment. It provides theoretical predictions for how adaptive reward mechanisms affect group payoff and cooperation, given game parameters such as group size, marginal per-capita return, and reward strength. However, it does not address punishment, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding the efficiency impact of reward-based interventions, especially when reward strength is allowed to adapt to cooperation levels.

- source: Kang, H. W., Jin, Z. Y., Li, M. Y., Wang, M., Sun, X. P., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). Discreet investors exert greater influence on cooperation in the public goods game. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The introduction of discreet investors—who pay a cost to obtain information about their neighbors' cooperation and adjust their investments accordingly—leads to higher average payoffs and more robust cooperation compared to standard cooperators, especially at low synergy factors (r). As the cost of information (C_DI) increases, the advantage of discreet investors diminishes, and at high synergy factors, standard cooperators can survive and dominate. The model does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcome is average payoff, which is a direct analog to efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide evidence that mechanisms allowing players to condition their contributions on local information (at a cost) can increase average payoffs and cooperation, especially when the cost is low. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant because it does not manipulate or analyze punishment. It may be useful as a conceptual analog for the effects of costly information acquisition or heterogeneity in investment, but not for punishment effects.

- source: Wang, J. W., Yu, F. Y., He, J. L., Chen, W., Xu, W. S., Dai, W. H., & Ming, Y. X. (2023). Promotion, Disintegration and Remediation of group cooperation under heterogeneous distribution system based on peer rating. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: Redistribution rules that reward willingness to cooperate (high alpha) and are neutral with respect to initial endowment (beta near zero) lead to the highest group efficiency (average payoff) and lowest inequality. Redistribution that favors the poor (negative beta) or the rich (positive beta) increases inequality and does not maximize group payoff. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning; all effects are due to redistribution of payoffs after the public goods game. The findings challenge the assumption that subsidizing the poor in such games always promotes cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. However, it is highly informative about how redistribution mechanisms (distinct from punishment) affect efficiency and cooperation in heterogeneous populations. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it does provide a useful contrast: redistribution based on willingness to cooperate can increase efficiency, while redistribution based on endowment does not. The game design dimensions are well specified for redistribution, but not for punishment.

- source: Wang, C. Q., Lin, Z. Z., & Rothman, D. S. (2022). Public goods game on coevolving networks driven by the similarity and difference of payoff. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the way agents update strategies—locally, globally, or a mix—has complex effects on cooperation and payoffs in spatial PGGs. When both cooperators and defectors have the same tendency for global updating, local updating is more beneficial for cooperation at low synergy factors (r), while both local and global updating can support cooperation at high r, but their combination can be detrimental. When cooperators and defectors differ in their global updating propensity, the system's cooperation and payoff outcomes depend sensitively on these propensities. Moderate global updating by defectors and low global updating by cooperators most favors cooperation and higher payoffs. The paper does not study punishment or its effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGGs. It is useful for understanding how information structure (local vs global updating) and network effects influence cooperation and payoffs, but does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled versus punishment-disabled efficiency. The absence of punishment mechanisms means it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Wang, J. W., Chen, W., Yu, F. Y., He, J. L., & Xu, W. S. (2022). Wealth-based rule favors cooperation in costly public goods games when individual selection is inevitable. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in both independent and interdependent PGGs with two cooperative strategies, the coexistence and prevalence of each strategy depend on the synergy factor and the difference in input costs. In the interdependent model, effective input and economic benefit are maximized when the two strategies are present in similar proportions. The interdependence mechanism (In-PGG) can sometimes lead to more quasi-cooperation than the independent mechanism (An-PGG), especially when the synergy factor and the difference in input costs are small. However, the paper does not directly report or quantify group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the stable proportions of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctions. It is useful for understanding how the structure of cooperation (independent vs interdependent strategies) and parameter settings affect the prevalence of cooperative behaviors, but it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes in a way that can be directly mapped to the prediction task. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how structural features of the game (such as interdependence of contributions) affect the emergence of cooperation, but not for the effect of punishment.

- source: Wang, Q. S., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2025). Coevolutionary dynamics of feedback-evolving games in structured populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in a public goods game with environmental investment is promoted when social attention (driven by individual attitudes and environmental feedback) is high and when the investment budget exceeds a threshold. The model identifies three types of public response mechanisms to environmental degradation, with the 'precautionary' mechanism (rapid response to early degradation) being most beneficial for cooperation. The level of cooperation is sensitive to the parameters governing attention and forgetfulness, but the level of social attention itself is robust to these parameters within certain intervals. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, and efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its findings are limited to the dynamics of cooperation and social attention in a PGG with environmental investment and evolving attitudes. It may provide indirect context for how non-punitive mechanisms (like attention or investment) affect cooperation, but is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Yang, Z. Q. L., Li, C. C., Wang, X., & Tian, Y. L. (2025). PPO-ACT: Proximal policy optimization with adversarial curriculum transfer for spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The PPO-ACT algorithm, which combines proximal policy optimization with adversarial curriculum transfer, enables higher and more robust cooperation rates in spatial public goods games compared to standard PPO, Q-learning, and Fermi update rules. PPO-ACT achieves stable all-cooperator states at lower enhancement factors (r) than other algorithms, and is robust to challenging initial conditions (e.g., all-defector starts). The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; no explicit analysis of group efficiency or total payoff is provided. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how advanced learning algorithms affect cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The design dimensions and results may be useful for modeling baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, but not for the downstream task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Zhang, L., Zhang, L. M., & Huang, C. W. (2022). Defectors in bad circumstances possessing higher reputation can promote cooperation. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a reputation rule—where reputation is based on matching one's strategy to one's neighbors—combined with heterogeneous investment allocation (parameterized by α), strongly promotes cooperation in spatial PGGs. Higher heterogeneity (larger α) lowers the critical synergy factor required for cooperation to emerge and increases the region of parameter space where full cooperation is stable. The results are robust to changes in network structure (square lattice, Moore neighborhood, scale-free network), total investment, noise, and initial cooperation proportion. However, the study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation and heterogeneous investment can promote cooperation, but not for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. It may be contextually useful for understanding alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

- source: Shi, Z. Y., Wei, W., Zheng, H. W., & Zheng, Z. M. (2023). Bidirectional supervision: An effective method to suppress corruption and defection under the third party punishment mechanism of donation games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when the weight of reputation in the strategy update rule is moderate, expanding the influence range of high-reputation individuals promotes cooperation (higher fraction of cooperators). When the reputation weight is low, expanding the influence range actually reduces cooperation. The effect of expanding influence range has diminishing returns (marginal effect). The model is a spatial PGG on a square lattice, with no punishment or reward mechanisms. Payoff is only discussed for subgroups and not as a main outcome. The findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are about the effect of reputation-based influence range on cooperation rates in a spatial PGG. While it provides insight into how information and influence structures can affect cooperation, it is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Wang, S., Li, Z., Li, W. D., & Jiang, H. (2022). Continuous Spatial Public Goods Game Based on Particle Swarm Optimization with Memory Stability. *Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a continuous spatial PGG with PSO-based learning, the memory stability coefficient (α) has a complex effect on the average cooperation level. At low enhancement factors (r), high memory stability (large α) helps maintain a moderate level of cooperation, while reducing memory stability (lower α) leads to a collapse of cooperation. At high r, reducing memory stability (lower α) allows the system to forget low-investment strategies and promotes the formation of high-cooperation clusters, increasing the average cooperation level. The effect of memory stability is more pronounced when players rely more on their own history (high ω) than on neighbor imitation. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present. The findings are relevant for understanding how learning rules and memory affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The results may be indirectly useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatial, continuous PGGs with PSO-type learning, but cannot inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Kang, H. W., Jiang, C., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). Neighbor-aware reinforcement learning fosters cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that integrating neighbor-aware reinforcement learning (NI) and reputation mechanisms in a spatial public goods game model can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators and promote the formation of stable cooperator clusters. The effect is especially strong when agents can observe a wider neighborhood (larger perceptual range). Reputation alone can promote cooperation if it is sufficiently weighted in the reward function, but the combination of NI and reputation produces a synergistic effect. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms in the sense of costly peer or institutional sanctions/rewards. The main dependent variable is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how social learning, reputation, and local information can promote cooperation, but not for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment. The model and results may be useful for understanding moderators of cooperation in spatial and learning-based PGGs, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

  findings: The paper finds that longer memory (up to a threshold) and slower memory decay promote higher levels of cooperation in both scale-free and small-world networks. - source: Stock, E. V., Valverde, P., González-Avella, J. C., Iglesias, J. R., Gonçalves, S., & da Silva, R. (2025). Cyclical oscillations and absorbing-state probabilities in optional public goods games: Interplay of reward and group size. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the OPGG exhibits rich dynamical behavior, including cyclic dominance among strategies and multistability in absorbing states, depending on the multiplication factor (r) and group size (S). For r < 2, loners dominate; for r > S, cooperators dominate; and for 2 < r < S, the system can end in any of the three absorbing states, with the likelihood and duration of cycles depending on S and r. Larger group sizes and higher r values tend to favor cooperation, but the model does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide detailed evidence on how group size and the multiplication factor affect the stability and dynamics of cooperation, defection, and abstention in optional public goods games. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide useful context for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment.

However, the paper does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report on efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes.

It is useful for understanding computational methods and the dynamics of cooperation in standard PGGs, but since it does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes, it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects. It may be referenced for baseline PGG design and simulation methodology, but not for treatment effect prediction.

- source: Xie, K., Liu, X. W., Chen, H., & Yang, J. (2022). Preferential selection and expected payoff drive cooperation in spatial voluntary public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial VPGG, introducing an expected payoff threshold and preferential selection in strategy updating can significantly increase the cooperation rate under certain parameter regimes. Specifically, positive expected payoff thresholds (p_e) and higher multiplication factors (r) promote cooperation, while the effect of the preferential selection parameter (a) depends on its sign and value. The cooperation rate is robust to a for positive p_e, but both a and r matter for negative p_e. The main dependent variable is the cooperation rate; no efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.

For prediction tasks focused on punishment or efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be contextually useful for understanding how non-punishment mechanisms (like aspiration levels and neighbor selection) affect cooperation rates.

- source: Xie, K., & Szolnoki, A. (2025). Reputation in public goods cooperation under double Q-learning protocol. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that integrating reputation into both investment decisions and payoff calculations, especially when using double Q-learning, substantially increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games. The effect is strongest when the weight of reputation in payoff (η), the synergy factor (r), and the reputation sensitivity parameter (δ) are all sufficiently high. The model demonstrates that cooperation can be sustained even in challenging environments, but only when these parameters are jointly favorable. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rate, reputation distribution).

- source: Lv, S. J., Li, J. Y., & Zhao, C. H. (2025). Reinforcement learning in spatial public goods games with environmental feedbacks. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that both higher synergy coefficient (r) and stronger environmental feedback (alpha) promote higher levels of cooperation in the SPGG. The effect of the Q-learning discount factor (gamma) on cooperation is non-monotonic and depends on the values of r and alpha. When r or alpha is low, higher gamma (more future-oriented agents) promotes cooperation; when r and alpha are high, lower gamma (more myopic agents) is more favorable for cooperation. The results are based on agent-based simulations and focus on the fraction of cooperators and spatial clustering, not on payoff or efficiency outcomes. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how environmental feedback and reinforcement learning affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based outcomes or for the effect of punishment. The game design dimensions covered (e.g., player count, mpcr, rounds, absence of punishment/reward) may be useful for contextualizing baseline cooperation in spatial PGGs with environmental feedback, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Xu, Z. H., Liu, X., Wang, H. N., Cui, L. Q., Han, X. P., & Meng, F. Y. (2024). Free-rider or contributor: A dilemma in spatial threshold public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

Key findings are: (1) Higher synergy factors (multipliers) increase cooperation; (2) Moderate fractions of late movers can enhance cooperation, but too many may reduce it; (3) Higher thresholds for game activation promote cooperation; (4) Clustering of late movers spatially can further increase cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.

For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in threshold PGGs without punishment.

- source: Wang, J. W., Dai, W. H., Zheng, Y. F., Yu, F. Y., Chen, W., & Xu, W. S. (2024). Partial intervention promotes cooperation and social welfare in regional public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

- source: Wang, X. Y., He, Z. X., & Shi, L. (2024). Conformity poses a double-edged sword effect on the evolution of cooperation within heterogeneous endowment populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the existence and maintenance of pure cooperator groups in a multi-group public goods game can promote the rise of cooperation in the population, even without mechanisms like punishment or reward. However, mutation and migration allow defectors to invade cooperator groups, undermining cooperation. Group division (splitting of large groups) can counteract the negative effects of mutation by generating new cooperator groups, but only if mutation rates are not too high and group division is sufficiently frequent. The synergy factor (r) and contribution level (c) also moderate these dynamics. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly transferable to payoff-based prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it provides indirect context on how group structure, mutation, and migration affect cooperation rates. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as a reminder that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., group division, migration) can strongly affect cooperation, and that the presence or absence of such mechanisms should be considered when interpreting or predicting the effects of punishment.

- source: Quan, J., Li, H. Z., Zhang, M., & Wang, X. J. (2024). Cooperation dynamics in nonlinear spatial public goods games with endogenous synergy and discounting feedback. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that introducing endogenous feedback—where the payoff structure depends on the current and historical level of cooperation—promotes the emergence and stability of cooperation in spatial public goods games. Both the length and intensity of the feedback mechanism (how much history is considered and how strongly it affects payoffs) increase the fraction of cooperators in the population. The model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The results are based on simulations of cooperation density and strategy dynamics.

Its findings on endogenous feedback and nonlinear payoffs may be contextually relevant for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Li, Y. P., Hu, X. Y., Jin, X., Zhang, H. Z., Yang, J. J., & Wang, Z. (2025). Environmental information perception enhances cooperation in stochastic public goods games via Q-learning. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a stochastic PGG with environmental feedback, the ability of individuals to perceive environmental state information significantly promotes the emergence and stability of cooperation. Specifically, the critical synergy factor (r) required for cooperation to emerge is lower when individuals can perceive the environment, compared to both a static environment and a dynamic environment that is not perceived. The study uses Q-learning agents on spatial networks and explores a variety of model extensions (update rules, neighborhood size, network topology, etc.), consistently finding that environmental information perception lowers the cooperation threshold. However, the paper does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not include any punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are adjacent to the prediction task, as they show that environmental information perception (not punishment) can promote cooperation (not efficiency) in stochastic PGGs. The results may be useful for understanding how information and feedback mechanisms affect cooperation thresholds, but they do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative.

- source: Wang, J. W., Chen, W., Yu, F. Y., Zhou, S. Y., He, J. L., Xu, W. S., & Dai, W. H. (2024). The emergence of cooperation in the context of prior agreement with threshold and posterior compensation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game where only the top V individuals by cumulative wealth can participate and must pay a participation cost, the effect of participation cost on cooperation depends on V. For small V, increasing participation cost can promote cooperation up to a point but increases wealth polarization. For large V, increasing participation cost slightly inhibits cooperation but reduces polarization. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism studied.

However, it may provide indirect context for how participation costs and selection mechanisms affect cooperation and wealth distribution. It is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Flores, L. S., Vainstein, M. H., Fernandes, H. C. M., & Amaral, M. A. (2023). Heterogeneous contributions can jeopardize cooperation in the public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study demonstrates that in spatial PGGs with imitation dynamics, there is an optimal homogeneous contribution value that maximizes cooperation (measured as cooperator density) for a given lattice structure. Introducing heterogeneity in contributions (i.e., allowing cooperators to contribute different amounts) can reduce the overall level of cooperation compared to this optimum, due to parasitic interactions where higher-contribution cooperators are exploited by lower-contribution ones. The effect of heterogeneity depends on the lattice structure: on square lattices, heterogeneity is detrimental to cooperation at the optimum, while on triangular lattices (with clustering), the effect is less negative. The main dependent variable is the equilibrium density of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in contributions (a form of player heterogeneity) affects cooperation rates, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes. The results may be useful for contextualizing the impact of contribution heterogeneity as a moderator, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment. The absence of punishment means this paper should not be used as primary evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Alfaro, G., & Sanjuan, M. A. F. (2022). Time-dependent effects hinder cooperation on the public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game model, moderate levels of habitat destruction increase the fraction of cooperators, while high levels cause cooperation to collapse. The multiplication factor (r) increases cooperation monotonically. The model does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators in the population.

- source: Lu, Z. Y., Hua, S. J., Wang, L. C., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Hybrid reward-punishment in feedback-evolving game for common resource governance. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a feedback-evolving common resource game (a close variant of the public goods game), the introduction of punishment (or a hybrid of reward and punishment) by a third-party institution significantly increases the likelihood of achieving both full cooperation and sustainable resource levels, which are direct analogs to high efficiency. Pure reward is less effective than punishment or hybrid schemes, especially when the resource growth rate is moderate. Increasing the per capita incentive (tax) and allocating more of the incentive budget to punishment (lower w) both make the ideal outcome (full cooperation and resource sustainability) more likely. When the resource growth rate is too low, no incentive scheme is effective. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are supported by both analytical and numerical phase diagrams.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in environments where resource renewal is not too slow, adding punishment (or a hybrid of reward and punishment) increases the likelihood of achieving high efficiency (full cooperation and resource sustainability) compared to reward alone or no incentives. The effect is stronger when the per capita incentive is higher and when more of the incentive budget is allocated to punishment. The model is deterministic and infinite-population, but the qualitative insights about the superiority of punishment (or hybrid) over pure reward for efficiency are likely to generalize to similar institutional public goods settings. The paper does not address stochasticity, finite groups, or experimental data, but its results are directly relevant for theoretical prediction.

- source: Vinayak, R. K. (2025). FARMER'S GAME: A GAME THEORETIC APPROACH TOWARDS BURNING OF CROP RESIDUE. *Journal of Dynamics and Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that, without intervention, individual incentives lead to inefficient outcomes (universal burning of crop residue), analogous to the tragedy of the commons. Social pressure can sometimes induce cooperation, but is often not strong enough. The introduction of government punishment (penalty per burned plot) and/or reward (per non-burned plot) can shift the Nash equilibrium to full cooperation (no burning), maximizing group payoff and efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions for when this shift occurs: when the sum of penalty and reward per plot exceeds the net private benefit of burning minus the greener alternative and pollution cost. Simulations confirm these theoretical results, showing that moderate levels of punishment and reward are sufficient to achieve efficient, cooperative outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that introducing punishment (and/or reward) in a public-goods-like environment with negative externalities can shift the equilibrium from inefficient (all defect) to efficient (all cooperate), provided the policy parameters are set so that the penalty plus reward per unit exceeds the net private benefit of defection. The model is directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency: if the punishment is sufficiently strong (relative to the private incentive to defect), efficiency will increase dramatically, potentially reaching full efficiency. The findings are most applicable to settings where punishment is implemented as a per-action cost, is known to all, and is not so severe as to cause practical or political infeasibility. The model does not address empirical implementation issues, but provides clear guidance on the direction and mechanism of efficiency change.

- source: Wang, L. C., Hua, S. J., Liu, Y. Y., Zhang, L., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Threshold incentive mechanisms for the sustainable management of public resources. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that threshold-based punishment and reward mechanisms can effectively promote cooperation and sustainable management of public resources, leading to higher efficiency (resource sustainability and group payoff) at lower cost than continuous incentives. Punishment is only triggered when the number of cooperators exceeds a threshold, targeting defectors, while reward is triggered when cooperation is low or resources are depleted. These mechanisms can stabilize the system at higher resource levels and higher cooperation rates, but their effectiveness is limited if the resource's natural growth rate is too low or the initial resource stock is too small. The models also show that increasing population size can reduce equilibrium resource levels, and that the interplay of reward/punishment thresholds and strengths is important for optimal outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that threshold-triggered punishment (and reward) mechanisms in public-goods-like resource games can increase efficiency relative to no incentives, especially when the resource can regenerate and initial conditions are not too dire. The effect of punishment is more positive for efficiency when it is targeted (threshold-based) rather than universal, and when combined with reward mechanisms. The model's explicit tracking of average payoffs and resource levels makes its results directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with similar design dimensions. However, the results are theoretical and may not capture all real-world behavioral nuances.

- source: Wang, J. F., & Shen, A. Z. (2024). The synergy of elimination and zero-determinant strategy on dynamic games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that implementing endogenous reward and punishment policies in a regional public goods game increases both cooperation and total social welfare (W_t), especially when the intervention is targeted (partial intervention) rather than universal. The efficiency gains from punishment are largest when the cost of policy implementation is low or the punishment is strong. There is often an optimal fraction of groups to intervene in, beyond which additional intervention yields diminishing or negative returns due to policy costs. Size-based selection of groups for intervention is generally superior to random or degree-based selection. Allowing non-intervened groups to spontaneously adopt the policy can further boost cooperation and welfare, but only if the additional costs are offset by increased benefits. Terminating intervention leads to rapid collapse of cooperation and welfare, indicating the need for sustained policy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that enabling punishment (and reward) in a PGG-like environment increases efficiency (total social welfare), but the magnitude and even the sign of the effect depend on the cost of policy implementation, the strength of punishment, and the fraction of groups covered. For prediction, the model suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency relative to control, especially when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is not too low and policy costs are not too high. However, if policy costs are high or the environment is resource-poor (low mpcr), the efficiency gain may be small or even negative. The method of targeting intervention (e.g., size-based) can further moderate the effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG variants with spatial or regional structure and endogenous institutional punishment.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2024). Impact of dynamic compensation with resource feedback on the common pool resource game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that (1) static punishment (compensation) above a critical threshold leads to full cooperation, resource sustainability, and maximum group payoff; (2) dynamic punishment with resource feedback can promote cooperation and efficiency even when static punishment is insufficient, especially at low initial compensation intensity; (3) the relationship between punishment parameters and efficiency is non-monotonic and context-dependent, with oscillatory or convergent dynamics depending on parameter regions; (4) the proportion of fines used for ecological compensation (vs. other uses) should be tuned to maximize efficiency, with all fines used for compensation when feedback is high or initial intensity is high, and none when both are low.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that increasing punishment intensity (compensation) above a critical threshold sharply increases efficiency (group payoff) in common pool resource games. Dynamic, feedback-based punishment can further improve efficiency, especially when static punishment is weak. The results suggest that, for prediction, the presence and strength of punishment, as well as whether it is dynamically adjusted based on resource feedback, are key moderators of efficiency gains. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially for settings with resource feedback, variable punishment intensity, and allocation of fines to resource restoration.

- source: Cooney, D. B. (2025). Exploring the Evolution of Altruistic Punishment with a PDE Model of Cultural Multilevel Selection. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that altruistic punishment, when combined with group-level selection, can promote higher levels of cooperation and increase group efficiency compared to scenarios without punishment. However, the effect of punishment on efficiency is sensitive to the cost structure (per-interaction vs. fixed), the strength of punishment, and the form of group-level competition. In some models, increasing punishment strength or cost leads to higher cooperation but lower efficiency, due to the collective cost of punishment outweighing its benefits. The paper provides analytical expressions for the threshold group selection strength needed to sustain cooperation and for the steady-state average payoff, showing that these can depend non-monotonically on punishment parameters. The results highlight that the design of punishment mechanisms and the structure of group competition are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, mechanistic, and quantitative guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments with group structure. It shows that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the cost and strength of punishment, the type of cost (per-interaction or fixed), and the form of group-level competition. For prediction, the results suggest that: (1) punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when costs are per-interaction rather than fixed; (2) increasing punishment strength generally increases cooperation, but may decrease efficiency if costs are high; (3) the effect of punishment on efficiency can be non-monotonic, especially under group-level competition rules that normalize by maximum/minimum group payoffs; (4) the threshold group selection strength required for cooperation is lower with stronger or cheaper punishment. The paper's analytical formulas and numerical results can be used to estimate treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design parameters, especially in settings with group-structured populations and multilevel selection.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Chen, Z., Perc, M., Wang, Z., Kurths, J., & Moreno, Y. (2023). Deterrence through punishment can resolve collective risk dilemmas in carbon emission games. *Chaos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment simulating a collective-risk public goods game (carbon emission game), costly punishment of free-riders (with fines imposed probabilistically and costs shared by all group members) increases the probability that groups reach the collective target, but only when the punishment risk is high (p > 0.8). At low punishment risk, deterrence is weak, and group failure rates remain high. Larger groups are less likely to succeed at any given punishment risk, requiring higher punishment risk for similar deterrence. The introduction of punishment shifts most players to fair-share contributions, reducing both free-riding and maximal altruism. The effect of punishment on group success (a proxy for efficiency) is robust to different loss probabilities. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, but the probability of reaching the target and average investment are closely related and show a positive effect of punishment when sufficiently strong.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in collective-risk public goods games with a threshold target and probabilistic costly punishment, enabling punishment increases group success rates (and thus efficiency) only when the punishment risk is high. The effect is moderated by group size (larger groups require higher punishment risk) and is robust to different loss probabilities. For prediction, the presence and strength of punishment (punishment risk, fine size, cost structure), group size, and the absence of communication are key moderators. The control (no-punishment) condition yields low group success rates, especially as group size increases. The findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency when punishment is strong and credible, but little or no effect when punishment is weak or infrequent.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2023). Governance of risky public goods under the threat of ostracism. *Physica D-nonlinear Phenomena*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a collective risk dilemma (CRD) model, local exclusion (ostracism) is more effective than local punishment in promoting group achievement (i.e., the fraction of groups that reach the collective target), especially at low risk levels. Local sanctioning institutions (both punishment and exclusion) are more effective than global ones. The advantage of exclusion over punishment is most pronounced when the risk of collective failure is low. When risk is high, both exclusion and punishment lead to high group achievement. The results are based on theoretical analysis and simulation of evolutionary dynamics, not on direct measurement of efficiency or group payoff, but group achievement is a closely related outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in threshold public goods environments with risk of collective loss (CRD), enabling local punishment or exclusion mechanisms increases group achievement (a proxy for efficiency) compared to no sanctions, with local exclusion (ostracism) being especially effective at low risk. The results suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment (especially local, exclusionary forms) is likely to increase efficiency or group success, particularly when the risk of failure is not too high. However, the findings are based on group achievement rather than explicit efficiency ratios, and are derived from theoretical models rather than empirical data. The model parameters (e.g., group size, cost of exclusion, risk level) are specified, allowing some mapping to prediction dimensions.

- source: Esmaeili, P., Makui, A., Seyedhosseini, S. M., & Ghousi, R. (2022). The effect of probabilistic incentives to promote cooperation during the pandemics using simulation of multi-agent evolutionary game. *International Journal of Industrial Engineering Computations*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that probabilistic third-party incentives (reward and punishment) can trigger abrupt transitions from low to high cooperation in a networked volunteer dilemma game. Pure punishment is more effective than pure reward, but a combination of both (with a higher share for reward) is optimal for promoting cooperation at lower monitoring costs. The main reported outcome is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings are based on agent-based simulations and catastrophe theory analysis, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the presence and probability of punishment (and reward) can cause sharp increases in cooperation rates in volunteer dilemma games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that punishment (especially when combined with reward) can be a powerful lever for increasing cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency in similar public goods settings, but this is not directly demonstrated. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of incentive mechanisms and the potential for nonlinear effects, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Shuvo, M. F. R., & Kabir, K. M. A. (2024). Exploring cyclic dominance in rock-paper-scissor strategies within the Optional Prisoner's Dilemma: A quantitative analysis with environmental feedback. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment increases cooperation in one-shot, three-person PD games, both when imposed exogenously (costless) and endogenously (costly to the punisher). The effect of punishment is decomposed into a direct effect (altering incentives for the punished individual) and an indirect effect (raising expectations that others will cooperate, thus encouraging conditional cooperators). Both effects are of similar magnitude. In the endogenous punishment condition, the cost of punishment is small relative to the benefit from increased cooperation, resulting in a net positive benefit. However, the main outcome is contribution, not group efficiency or total payoff, though some calculations of net benefit are provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (even when costly to the punisher) increases cooperation in public-goods-like environments, and that the effect is due to both direct and indirect mechanisms. While efficiency (as defined by group payoff relative to maximum possible) is not directly reported, the paper's calculations of net benefit and cost-effectiveness suggest that punishment increases group welfare. The design is a one-shot, three-player, continuous-contribution PD with and without punishment, and with both exogenous and endogenous punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when both direct and indirect effects are present, and when punishment is not prohibitively costly. However, the lack of direct efficiency reporting means that predictions must be inferred from contribution and net benefit data.

- source: Yan, R. Y., Xu, M. Q., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). Evolution of cooperation under pure institutional reward and punishment in collective-risk social dilemma games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing institutional pure punishment or pure reward into a collective-risk social dilemma game can promote cooperation, but with important differences. Pure reward leads to stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, with the domain of coexistence expanding as reward intensity increases. Pure punishment produces more complex dynamics, including monostable, bistable, tristable, and quadristable outcomes, and can more effectively suppress free-riding when punishment intensity is high or the implementation threshold is low. In finite populations, lower thresholds for implementing incentives (reward or punishment) promote cooperation, while higher thresholds favor defection. The cost and intensity of incentives modulate these effects. However, the paper does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the evolutionary stability and prevalence of cooperative strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of institutional punishment on cooperation in collective-risk public goods games, especially regarding how punishment intensity, cost, and implementation threshold affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence on group efficiency or total payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to inferring that higher cooperation rates (under certain punishment regimes) are likely to be associated with higher efficiency, assuming the mapping from cooperation to efficiency is known. The results are most relevant for settings with institutional (not peer) punishment, nonlinear threshold public goods, and no direct cost of cooperation for punishers/rewarders. The findings suggest that lower punishment thresholds and higher punishment intensity (relative to cost) are more likely to promote cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if cooperation is strongly linked to group payoff.

- source: Wang, Z., Li, R. D., Jin, X., & Ding, H. (2023). Emergence of Social Norms in Metanorms Game With High-Order Interaction Topology. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in metanorm games on uniform random hypergraphs (URH), increasing group size (G) promotes the emergence of social norms, leading to higher cooperation rates and more agents willing to punish defectors. Network connectivity (number of groups) does not affect norm emergence. On heterogeneous hypergraphs (HRH), standard BV-learning fails to establish norms for 'leaver' agents, but a new dynamic relevance BV-learning algorithm enables both hub and leaver agents to establish norms and cooperate. Real-world hypergraph data (coauthorship networks) show similar patterns. The main findings are about the conditions for norm emergence and high cooperation, not about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of punishment on cooperation and norm compliance in public-goods-like games with high-order (group) interactions, especially regarding the role of group size and network structure. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that larger group size and effective learning algorithms for punishment/metapunishment can promote cooperation and norm compliance, which may be associated with higher efficiency in related settings, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Zhang, W., Zhao, D. K., Jin, X., Zhang, H. Z., An, T. B., Cui, G. H., & Wang, Z. (2025). Q-learning facilitates norm emergence in metanorm game model with topological structures. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a metanorm game with punishment and metapunishment, agents using self-regarding Q-learning can achieve high and robust cooperation rates (over 90% in many cases), even when agents lack information about others' payoffs. Punishment and metapunishment are essential for maintaining cooperation and suppressing defection and non-punishment. The model is robust to changes in network topology (scale-free, random, small-world), temptation payoff, population size, and observability. Highly connected agents are more likely to adopt punitive strategies. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this work.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment and metapunishment mechanisms can sustain high cooperation rates in social dilemma environments with local interactions and limited information, especially when agents use reinforcement learning. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral and dynamic effects of punishment and metapunishment, and for anticipating that high cooperation is possible under a wide range of design parameters, but not for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Cheng, H. H., Meng, X. Z., Hayat, T., & Hobiny, A. (2023). Multistability and bifurcation analysis for a three-strategy game system with public goods feedback and discrete delays. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can promote the survival of cooperators and the abundance of public goods, but only when their intensities are sufficiently high. Moderate reward and severe punishment are most conducive to cooperation. Excessively high reward does not always further increase cooperation. The cost of reward has a stronger negative effect on cooperation than the cost of punishment. Initial conditions and time delays can lead to multistability, oscillations, or extinction of cooperation. Large delays are detrimental to cooperation and can lead to the extinction of cooperators. The model highlights the complex interplay between feedback, incentives, and dynamic stability in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and numerical evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation and public good abundance is positive when punishment is sufficiently strong and not too costly, especially when combined with moderate reward. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as supporting the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and public good provision under certain parameter regimes, but not as direct evidence for efficiency gains. The results suggest that the effect of punishment is sensitive to the costs of incentives, the presence of reward, and dynamic factors such as delays and initial conditions. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment can improve group outcomes in public-goods-like settings, but the mapping to efficiency is indirect.

- source: Qian, J., Sun, X., Zhang, T. D., & Chai, Y. T. (2022). Authority or Autonomy? Exploring Interactions between Central and Peer Punishments in Risk-Resistant Scenarios. *Entropy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that both central and peer punishment can promote group collaboration in risk-resistant (threshold) scenarios, with peer punishment generally having a stronger effect, especially as risk increases. Central punishment can suppress the effect of peer punishment at all risk levels, while peer punishment only constrains central punishment at high risk. Resource wastage from punishment is generally small compared to the loss from group failure, but increases with peer punishment intensity and risk. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff in the standard sense, focusing instead on collaboration rates and resource wastage.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It supports the idea that punishment (both central and peer) increases group collaboration in threshold public goods-like environments, and that the effectiveness and interaction of punishment types depend on risk level. However, because the main outcome is collaboration rate (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the model is theoretical/simulation-based rather than empirical, its direct predictive value for efficiency is limited. The resource wastage analysis suggests that high peer punishment can lead to diminishing returns or inefficiency at high risk. The model's all-or-nothing threshold structure and explicit manipulation of punishment cost and effectiveness are informative for mapping to game design dimensions.

- source: Chica, M., & Santos, F. C. (2023). Seeding leading cooperators and institutions in networked climate dilemmas. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in networked collective-risk dilemmas with local punishment institutions, seeding a small fraction of punishers (institution supporters) at random or in network hubs can shift the system from low to high group achievement and institution prevalence, even when initial conditions are otherwise unchanged. Seeding cooperators is much less effective. The presence of punishment institutions is necessary for high cooperation; network structure alone does not suffice. Seeding network hubs is more expensive but not more effective than random seeding. The main outcomes are group achievement and institution prevalence, not direct efficiency or payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment institutions (via seeding punishers) in a networked public-goods-like environment can strongly increase group-level cooperation outcomes (group achievement and institution prevalence), even when the control (no punishment) condition yields low cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, its findings should be used as informative but indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The results suggest that, in similar networked games, enabling punishment (especially via institutional mechanisms) is likely to increase efficiency if group achievement is a good proxy for efficiency. The paper also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment depends on both network structure and the presence of institutional mechanisms, and that seeding punishers is more effective than seeding cooperators.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Zhao, H., Liang, S., Li, L., & Song, Y. J. (2024). Inter-layer feedback mechanism with reinforcement learning boosts the evolution of cooperation in multilayer network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that adaptive punishment mechanisms, when combined with reinforcement learning in hypergraph-structured PGGs, substantially increase the fraction of cooperators. Key findings include: (1) higher punishment probability and intensity promote cooperation, (2) moderate punishment costs are optimal for sustaining cooperation, (3) smaller group sizes (hyperedges) facilitate earlier and higher cooperation, and (4) higher learning rates and discount factors in Q-learning further enhance cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that adaptive punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, especially when punishment is likely, strong, and not too costly, and when group sizes are small. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting how design dimensions (punishment cost, intensity, group size, etc.) affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency but are not equivalent. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect, mechanistic support for the positive effect of punishment on cooperation, but not direct evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wang, L. C., Liu, Y. Y., Guo, R. Q., Zhang, L., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). Cooperation and resource sustainability in coupling social-ecological systems with dynamic growth rates. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that a peer rating-based heterogeneous distribution system (PRHDS) can promote cooperation in a structured population, but is fragile to random exploration (strategy noise). The main threat is the invasion of egoistic cooperators, which undermines prosocial clusters. Introducing monetary punishments for minority raters (either as a fixed fine or as a reduction in distribution share) can restore and stabilize cooperation, making the system robust to low levels of noise. Non-monetary punishment (reducing the voice of minority raters) is ineffective. The effect of the system depends on the matching of the public goods multiplier and the weight of ratings in the distribution formula. The model is all-or-nothing in both contribution and rating, and the main reported outcomes are behavioral (cooperation rates, strategy fractions), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It models a close variant of the public goods game with a peer rating-based distribution system, which acts as a combined reward/punishment mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. However, the results suggest that monetary punishment mechanisms (with explicit costs or share reductions) can stabilize cooperation in noisy environments, while non-monetary punishments are ineffective. The model is all-or-nothing, with no chat, and the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is a key parameter. The findings suggest that, in similar designs, enabling monetary punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates (and thus, by implication, efficiency), especially when the system is subject to noise. The absence of direct efficiency outcomes limits the strength of inference for the prediction task.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2023). Evolution of spite in an n-player game with an opting-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated n-player games with an opting-out option, spiteful (costly harming) strategies can sometimes persist, but only under restrictive conditions: low cost-to-harm ratio, small group size, and specific opting-out rules. As group size increases, it becomes harder for spiteful strategies to achieve higher payoffs than nonspiteful strategies, and the basin of attraction for all-nonspiteful populations grows. The opting-out rule (how easily groups disband) is a key moderator: when homogeneous groups (all spiteful or all nonspiteful) are forced to disband, spite can persist more easily, but this is a somewhat artificial rule. The model is mathematically explicit about when spiteful strategies can dominate, and these conditions almost always correspond to lower group efficiency compared to all-nonspiteful populations. The analysis is directly about payoff and efficiency, not just behavior.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like n-player games with an opting-out mechanism, the introduction of costly punishment (here, spiteful harming) generally reduces group efficiency unless the cost-to-harm ratio is extremely low and the group size is small. As group size increases, the conditions for punishment/spite to persist become more restrictive, and group efficiency is more likely to be maximized when punishment/spite is absent. The details of the opting-out rule (e.g., whether homogeneous groups are forced to disband) can moderate this effect, but in most realistic settings, punishment/spite is not efficiency-enhancing. The model is not an exact PGG with standard punishment, but the payoff structure and evolutionary logic are closely related, and the results are directly about payoff/efficiency. Thus, the paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment/spite in large groups with standard cost structures will not increase efficiency, and may reduce it.

- source: Fontanari, J. F., & Santos, M. (2024). Solving the prisoner's dilemma trap in Hamilton's model of temporarily formed random groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
