  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the 'others-only' n-person prisoner's dilemma (a strong altruism scenario), cooperation cannot evolve under asexual reproduction or sexual reproduction with selfing, but can evolve and become stable under biparental sexual reproduction if the group benefit multiplier (r) is sufficiently large relative to group size (n) and the cost of cooperation (c) is not too high. The model does not include punishment or reward, and the main mechanism enabling cooperation is the genetic structure imposed by biparental reproduction. The main outcome is the probability that the population reaches the all-cooperator state (fixation probability), which is a direct measure of group efficiency or welfare. The results are summarized in phase diagrams showing regions where cooperation is stable, bistable, or unstable, as a function of c, r, and n. The findings are robust to demographic noise and extend to dioecious populations. The paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment, communication, or other institutional design features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments that lack punishment or reward mechanisms, especially when the game structure is the 'others-only' variant of the n-person prisoner's dilemma. It shows that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency (measured as fixation of cooperation and group payoff) is only achievable under specific genetic and demographic conditions—namely, biparental sexual reproduction, sufficiently high group benefit multiplier (r), and not-too-large group size (n) or cost (c). The results suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is generally low unless these conditions are met. However, the paper does not provide any evidence about the effect of enabling punishment or sanctions, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency difference between control and punishment-enabled conditions. Its main value is in establishing a baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is generally low except under the specified genetic/demographic conditions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Liu, Y., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2023). Evolutionary games with environmental feedbacks under an external incentive mechanism. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that external incentives (subsidies/carbon credits) can promote cooperation and improve environmental quality in a spatial public goods game with environmental feedback, but the effect is not always positive. Higher subsidies increase the competitiveness of cooperators (both those who reinvest in the public good and those who use credits for personal gain), but can also create conditions where defectors survive by exploiting improved environments, leading to coexistence or even dominance of defectors. The use of incentives to promote cooperation is thus sensitive to the balance between environmental repair and degradation rates, the structure of incentives, and the spatial dynamics of strategy clusters. The model does not include punishment or sanctions in the standard sense, but the 'second-order social dilemma' (where some cooperators act as free-riders on others' altruism) is analogous to the problem of sustaining costly punishment. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and environmental quality, with payoffs determined by these variables.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of external incentives (akin to rewards) on cooperation and group welfare in spatial public goods games with environmental feedback. It suggests that the effect of such mechanisms on efficiency or payoff is highly context-dependent: increasing the incentive can improve cooperation and welfare in some parameter regions, but can also reduce them in others due to complex spatial and strategic dynamics. The findings caution against assuming monotonic positive effects of incentives and highlight the importance of considering environmental feedbacks and the possibility of second-order free-riding. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctions as defined in the prediction task, so its direct applicability to punishment effects is limited. The results are most relevant for reward-based or subsidy-based interventions in spatial, environmentally-coupled PGGs.

- source: Chen, W., Zhu, Q. L., & Wu, T. (2023). Unfairness promotes the evolution of cooperation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reject unfair resource allocations (interpreted as a form of punishment) leads to higher levels of cooperation, especially when moderate unfairness is present. Both extreme fairness and extreme unfairness are suboptimal for cooperation. The mechanism is explained by cyclic dominance among strategy types. In continuous strategy settings, cooperators tend to act fairer than defectors, and clusters of cooperators can resist exploitation. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but rather focuses on the fraction of cooperators and the evolution of fairness/unfairness strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the ability to reject unfair offers (a form of costly punishment) can promote cooperation in public-goods-like games, especially when resource allocation is neither perfectly fair nor extremely unfair. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is also theoretical and does not manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions used in the prediction task. Thus, while the paper supports the general idea that punishment for unfairness can promote cooperation, it does not provide direct quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in empirical PGG experiments.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, X. M., Chen, W. M., & Wang, X. J. (2023). Cooperation dynamics in collective risk games with endogenous endowments. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a two-stage PGG-CRG model with endogenous endowments, higher perceived risk and lower required contribution ratios increase the proportion of the population that successfully avoids catastrophic loss (target achieved rate). The effect of the first-stage enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) is non-monotonic: when the required contribution ratio is small, higher enhancement factors help, but at moderate contribution ratios, a moderate enhancement factor is optimal. The model does not include or analyze punishment or sanction mechanisms, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or collective risk games, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in the model. However, it does provide informative context on how other game design parameters (risk, contribution ratio, enhancement factor) affect cooperation and group success in a threshold public goods/collective risk environment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency or cooperation rates in the absence of punishment.

- source: Ando, H., Nishi, A., & Handcock, M. S. (2025). Statistical modelling of networked evolutionary public goods games. *Journal of the Royal Statistical Society Series A-statistics in Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that participants preferentially form and maintain network ties with others who also cooperate, and avoid forming ties that would close triangles (i.e., transitive closure is disfavored in tie formation). Participants also prefer to connect with others of similar wealth. The modeling approach (STERGM) is validated for small, repeated networked public goods games. However, the experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. It does, however, provide detailed information about a networked PGG variant with dynamic partner selection, which may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation and network effects in the absence of punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency outcomes, this paper offers only contextual or indirect value.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., Gu, C. L., & Chen, W. M. (2024). Evolutionary dynamics in spatial public goods games with environmental feedbacks. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study models a spatial public goods game with environmental feedback, where individuals can be cooperators (who pay a cost to improve local environmental quality) or defectors (who do not pay and degrade the environment). The model explores how the ability of cooperators to repair the environment (delta), the destructiveness of defectors (gamma), and the cost of cooperation (c) affect the prevalence of cooperation. Key findings include: (1) Cooperation can only dominate if cooperator repair ability slightly exceeds defector destructiveness; (2) Increasing cooperator repair ability beyond a threshold can paradoxically reduce cooperation due to defector invasions; (3) Both very low and very high defector destructiveness can undermine cooperation; (4) Increasing cooperation cost generally reduces cooperation, but at low defector destructiveness, higher cost can sometimes help cooperation by reducing isolated cooperators and making clusters more robust. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide detailed theoretical insights into how environmental feedback, spatial structure, and cost parameters affect the evolution of cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide contextual understanding of how non-punishment mechanisms and environmental feedbacks shape cooperation dynamics in spatial PGG variants.

- source: Wang, X. J., Ding, R., Zhao, J. H., Chen, W. M., & Gu, C. L. (2022). Competition of punishment and reward among inequity-averse individuals in spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that reputation-based indirect reciprocity can promote the spread of donation and cooperation behaviors in a donation game variant of the PGG, but this effect is limited by the costs of donation and cooperation, and by the presence of 'hypocrite' and 'realist' strategies. When only extreme altruists and egoists are present, cooperation rarely persists. The introduction of shunners (who conditionally cooperate based on reputation) allows for the spread of donation and cooperation under certain cost regimes. However, the addition of hypocrites (who donate for reputation but defect in daily interactions) and realists (who do not donate but cooperate in daily interactions) undermines the prevalence of donation, leading to more complex phase diagrams and sometimes the collapse of donation behavior. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of different strategies and the qualitative structure of phase transitions; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as it explicitly excludes punishment mechanisms and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the role of indirect reciprocity (reputation) as an alternative to punishment in promoting cooperation, and about how the presence of certain strategy types and cost parameters can affect the prevalence of prosocial behaviors. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., reputation) can support cooperation under some conditions, but also that the presence of exploitative or non-donating cooperators can undermine these effects. The findings are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Liu, Y. Y., Liu, L. J., Guo, R. Q., & Zhang, L. (2023). N-player repeated evolutionary trust game under government management. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a government-managed punishment and reward mechanism in a repeated N-player trust game can sustain trust and investment, leading to higher group payoffs. There is a critical threshold of punishment intensity: below it, untrustworthy behavior dominates and payoffs are low; above it, trust and investment are maintained, and payoffs increase. The model also shows that as punishment intensity increases further, the system can reach a stable coexistence of strategies with high trust and investment. The results are robust across different group sizes and parameter values, and are supported by both theoretical analysis and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated trust-game-like environments with group structure, introducing or increasing the intensity of punishment (and associated rewards for reporting) can substantially increase group efficiency/payoff, provided the punishment is above a critical threshold. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, so the qualitative prediction that punishment increases efficiency is likely to transfer to PGG-like settings, especially those with repeated interactions and third-party enforcement. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented by an external authority and is contingent on reporting.

- source: Li, Y. Z., Luo, J., Niu, H., & Ye, H. (2023). When punishers might be loved: fourth-party choices and third-party punishment in a delegation game. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: Across three lab experiments, the paper shows: (1) Helpers are more trusted and preferred as partners in trust games, but punishers are more likely to be offered higher amounts in ultimatum games due to their deterrence signal, resulting in higher actual payoffs for punishers. (2) In a delegation game, when the proposer's knowledge of the delegate's punishment history is made explicit, fourth-party players prefer to select punishers as their delegates, and this choice leads to higher expected payoffs for both the delegate and the principal. (3) The effect is contingent on the visibility of the punishment signal; when the proposer's information is limited, helpers are preferred. Thus, punishment increases efficiency/payoff in environments where its deterrence is visible and relevant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in games adjacent to public goods games (third-party punishment, ultimatum, and delegation games), enabling punishment increases the efficiency (payoff) of punishers and those who select them as delegates, especially when the punishment signal is visible to others. The effect is context-dependent: punishment is less effective in trust-based settings but more effective in settings where deterrence is valuable and observable. For prediction tasks, the presence of a visible punishment mechanism, the structure of the game (ultimatum/delegation vs. trust), and the information available to other players are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is lower than with punishment in deterrence-relevant settings.

- source: Herne, K., Lappalainen, O., Setälä, M., & Ylisalo, J. (2022). Accountability as a Warrant for Trust: An Experiment on Sanctions and Justifications in a Trust Game. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment used a 2x2 design (punishment/no punishment x justification/no justification) in a three-player trust game (two senders, one responder, repeated stranger matching, 6 rounds). The opportunity for senders to punish responders (at a cost: 1 point spent reduces responder's payoff by 3) did not significantly increase the amounts sent or returned, nor did it improve group efficiency. In fact, net earnings for responders were lower in punishment treatments due to the cost of punishment, and group efficiency was not improved. Justification requirements (responders must explain their choices) increased contributions and returns, but this effect was not robust when controlling for the amount received. The combination of punishment and justification did not outperform justification alone in terms of efficiency. The main effect of punishment was to reduce responder payoffs via costly sanctions, not to increase group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a trust-game-like environment with costly punishment (punishment cost 1:3), enabling punishment does not increase group efficiency and may reduce it due to the cost of punishment. The design is adjacent to public goods games (not a standard PGG, but a trust game with two senders and one responder, continuous contributions, no communication, 6 rounds, known horizon, no rewards). The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be lower than or similar to the control (no punishment), unless the punishment is costless or much more effective. The paper also shows that justification requirements can increase contributions, but this does not translate into higher efficiency when punishment is present. Thus, for prediction, enabling costly punishment in similar trust or PGG-adjacent games is likely to reduce or not improve efficiency relative to control.

- source: Lec, F. L., Matthey, A., & Rydval, O. (2023). Punishing the weakest link - Voluntary sanctions and efficient coordination in the minimum effort game. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that voluntary costly punishment opportunities in a repeated minimum effort game (with 8 players per group, 16 rounds, and a two-stage design) significantly increase both effort levels and group efficiency compared to a baseline with no punishment and a treatment with costless disapproval (communication). In the punishment treatment, after an initial drop, average group payoff (net of punishment costs) rises and eventually exceeds the control, with about a third of groups reaching or approaching the Pareto-optimal equilibrium. Punishment is mainly directed at minimum-effort players, and its use is frequent. Disapproval/communication alone produces only a temporary or weak improvement. The results show that decentralized sanctions can serve as an effective coordination device in Pareto-ranked coordination games, with a net positive effect on efficiency in the longer run.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated weakest-link (minimum effort) games with large groups and a history of inefficient coordination, enabling voluntary costly punishment leads to a robust and lasting increase in group efficiency, even after accounting for the direct costs of punishment. The effect is stronger and more persistent than that of costless communication. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, holding control efficiency and other design dimensions constant, enabling punishment (with a 2:1 fine-to-cost ratio and anonymous assignment) will increase average efficiency, especially in settings with multiple rounds, large groups, and no prior communication. The initial rounds after introducing punishment may see a drop in net efficiency, but this reverses as groups coordinate on higher efforts. The results are most directly applicable to coordination games with similar structure, but provide adjacent evidence for public goods games.

- source: Gueth, W., & Otsubo, H. (2023). When Efficiency Requires Arbitrary Discrimination: Theoretical and Experimental Analysis of Equilibrium Selection. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that third-party discriminatory sanctioning (especially when preannounced and when agents differ in wealth) increases the likelihood that the two agents coordinate to avoid mutual harm, resulting in higher group efficiency. When the third party sanctions equally, agents are less likely to coordinate efficiently. The effect is robust to the order protocol (sequential vs. simultaneous), but is strongest when the sanctioning schedule is observable. Asymmetric wealth increases the tendency for the third party to sanction the richer agent more, confirming the 'deep-pocket' hypothesis. Overall, the ability to use discriminatory punishment, and to preannounce it, enables higher efficiency in this coordination dilemma.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a coordination game adjacent to the public goods game, enabling a third party to use discriminatory punishment (especially when the punishment schedule is observable) increases group efficiency by facilitating coordination and avoiding mutual harm. The findings suggest that, in similar environments, the design dimensions of punishment observability and the ability to discriminate in punishment can be leveraged to improve efficiency. The results are most relevant for settings where a third party can precommit to a punishment schedule and where agents may differ in wealth or status. For prediction tasks, the presence of observable, flexible, and potentially discriminatory punishment is likely to increase efficiency relative to control conditions without such punishment.

- source: Calabuig, V., Jiménez-Jiménez, N., Olcina, G., & Rodriguez-Lara, I. (2024). Coordinated and uncoordinated punishment in a team investment game. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares coordinated and uncoordinated punishment in a team investment game with two investors and one allocator. Coordinated punishment (where both investors must punish to inflict a penalty) increases joint investment and allocator return relative to uncoordinated punishment. However, this does not translate into higher efficiency, as the increased frequency of joint punishment in the coordinated condition destroys the potential efficiency gains. The highest efficiency (sum of total payoffs) is observed in the no-punishment condition. There are no significant efficiency differences between coordinated and uncoordinated punishment treatments after accounting for punishment costs. Thus, punishment—regardless of its form—does not improve efficiency in this setting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a team investment game with binary investment and an allocator, enabling punishment (either coordinated or uncoordinated) reduces efficiency compared to a no-punishment baseline. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are: binary (all-or-nothing) investment, two investors and one allocator (asymmetric roles), fixed punishment cost, and varying punishment effectiveness. The findings suggest that, even if punishment increases cooperation or returns, the cost and frequency of punishment can offset these gains, leading to lower or unchanged efficiency. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency, and the specific form of punishment (coordinated vs uncoordinated) does not significantly alter this outcome in this design. The results are most directly applicable to asymmetric, binary-contribution, repeated games with fixed matching and no communication.

- source: Gioffré, A., & Tampieri, A. (2025). Community enforcement and the cost of cooperation. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that community enforcement via grim punishment can sustain efficient cooperation in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with random matching, provided the cost of cooperation is not too low or too high. Specifically, if the cost-to-benefit ratio exceeds a threshold (less than 0.5), punishment is always credible and cooperation is an equilibrium for any population size and discount factor. For any environment, there exists a 'target cost of cooperation' that enables coordination on cooperation and makes punishment effective. When the cost of cooperation is endogenous, players can use it as a coordination device to achieve efficient outcomes. The results are entirely theoretical and provide explicit payoff-based conditions for when punishment increases group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environments with random matching and private monitoring, the introduction of credible punishment (grim trigger) can increase group efficiency to the cooperative level, provided the cost of cooperation is within a specific range. The key moderators are the cost-to-benefit ratio, population size, and discount factor. The results suggest that, for a wide range of parameter values, enabling punishment will move efficiency from the defection baseline to the cooperative optimum. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially the cost of cooperation (analogous to MPCR in PGGs), population size, and the presence of punishment. However, the model is not a literal public goods game but an adjacent repeated PD, so some caution is warranted in direct quantitative transfer.

- source: Macleod, W. B., Lara, V. V., & Zehnder, C. (2025). Worker Empowerment and Subjective Evaluation: On Building an Effective Conflict Culture. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of enabling punishment (conflict) in a principal-agent game with subjective evaluation is highly context-dependent. In the baseline, communication, and agreement (code of conduct) experiments, allowing workers to punish managers for not paying bonuses does not increase efficiency: the costs of conflict outweigh any small increases in effort, and total surplus is lower or unchanged compared to the no-punishment condition. Workers and managers fail to coordinate on an efficiency-enhancing norm, and punishment is often misapplied. However, when conflicts are institutionalized through a formal grievance process (grievance experiment), workers and managers coordinate more on a pay-for-output norm, effort increases significantly, and total surplus is higher in the punishment-enabled condition. Thus, punishment can enhance efficiency, but only when supported by strong institutional framing and norm coordination.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is not uniform and is highly sensitive to the institutional environment, especially the presence of mechanisms that coordinate norms (such as a grievance procedure). In environments lacking such coordination, enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency due to the direct costs of conflict. In contrast, when a formal grievance process is present, punishment can increase both effort and efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of institutionalized conflict resolution and norm-coordination mechanisms should be considered a key moderator. Control efficiency (no-punishment) is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment) unless the institutional context is specified.

- source: Murase, Y. (2025). Costly punishment sustains indirect reciprocity under low defection detectability. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of costly punishment in sustaining cooperation depends critically on the detectability of defection. When defection is difficult to detect (i.e., high probability that defection is misperceived as cooperation), social norms that include costly punishment (CP norms) can be the most efficient ESS, achieving high cooperation and high average payoff. When defection is easy to detect, punishment is rarely efficient and often reduces payoffs. The model provides explicit boundaries for when punishment is efficient, and shows that punishment is most effective when it is rarely executed but strongly deters defection. Execution errors and high punishment costs shrink the parameter space where punishment is efficient. The results challenge previous theoretical work that found only a narrow efficiency margin for punishment, showing that under asymmetric detection errors, punishment can be broadly efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, parameterized theoretical predictions for the effect of punishment on efficiency in indirect reciprocity games with pairwise donation structure. While not a standard public goods game, the model is adjacent and the efficiency results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in environments where defection is hard to detect. The explicit formulas and phase diagrams allow mapping from game design dimensions (punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, error rates) and baseline efficiency to the expected efficiency with punishment enabled. The main moderator is the detectability of defection: when it is low, punishment can substantially increase efficiency; when high, punishment is unnecessary or even harmful. The results are most transferable to settings with pairwise interactions, reputation, and indirect reciprocity, but provide strong mechanistic insight for broader PGG-like environments.

- source: Dasgupta, N., & Musolesi, M. (2025). Investigating the impact of direct punishment on the emergence of cooperation in multi-agent reinforcement learning systems. *Autonomous Agents and Multi-agent Systems*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Direct punishment, when just punishment is net-rewarding, is effective at promoting cooperation and maximizing group efficiency in MARL populations facing social dilemmas. Populations with direct punishment (especially with partner selection) achieve the highest total group reward at equilibrium, outperforming third-party punishment and combined punishment mechanisms in terms of efficiency, despite achieving slightly lower cooperation rates. Third-party punishment and combined punishment mechanisms lead to higher cooperation but incur greater punishment costs, reducing overall efficiency. The presence of partner selection and reputation further enhances the effectiveness of direct punishment. If just punishment is costly (i.e., not net-rewarding), neither cooperation nor punishment emerges, and group efficiency collapses. The findings are robust to changes in population size.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in Iterated Prisoner's Dilemma environments with MARL agents, enabling direct punishment (with net-rewarding just punishment) increases group efficiency compared to no punishment. The effect is strongest when combined with partner selection and reputation. Third-party punishment and combined punishment mechanisms further increase cooperation but reduce efficiency due to higher punishment costs. The results suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and type of punishment, the reward structure for just punishment, and the availability of partner selection and reputation are key moderators of the efficiency effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in adjacent social dilemma games (e.g., IPD) with similar design features, especially in artificial agent populations.

- source: Melkonyan, T., Zeitoun, H., & Chater, N. (2022). The cognitive foundations of tacit commitments: A virtual bargaining model of dynamic interactions. *Journal of Mathematical Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in dynamic games without external enforcement, the possibility of tacit commitment to punishment (as formalized by the virtual bargaining equilibrium) enables self-interested agents to achieve efficient, mutually beneficial outcomes that are not predicted by standard subgame perfect equilibrium. In games like the finitely repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, Centipede, and repeated Bertrand competition, VBE supports cooperation, collusion, or turn-taking, enforced by credible threats of punishment (even if costly). This leads to higher group payoffs and efficiency compared to the standard prediction. However, if players cannot commit to future punishment (SVBE), the threat is not credible and efficiency collapses to the Nash/subgame perfect level.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling credible punishment (even if costly to the punisher) can substantially increase group efficiency in repeated or sequential social dilemma games, relative to control conditions where punishment is not possible or not credible. The model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, but the logic applies to adjacent environments (e.g., repeated PD, Centipede, repeated Bertrand) that share key features with public goods games. The findings suggest that, when game design allows for credible commitment to punishment, efficiency is likely to be much higher than in control games without such commitment. The effect is robust to the absence of external enforcement or non-standard preferences. However, the model assumes agents reason as virtual bargainers; if they do not, or if commitment is not possible, efficiency gains disappear. The paper is most informative for settings where punishment is possible, costly, and can be credibly pre-committed.

- source: Mondal, S. S., Pathak, M., & Chakraborty, S. (2022). Reward versus punishment: averting the tragedy of the commons in eco-evolutionary dynamics. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can prevent the tragedy of the commons in a dynamic resource game, but their effects on efficiency differ. Sufficiently strong punishment leads to a stable equilibrium with all cooperators and a fully replete resource, achieving the social optimum (maximum possible group payoff). In contrast, even with strong reward, some defectors persist, so the group does not reach the maximum efficiency. The model also shows that the effect of punishment or reward depends on the initial state and the intensity of the intervention, and that bistability (multiple possible long-run outcomes) can occur. The analysis is theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis, and is not based on empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in dynamic resource games with feedback (and by extension, in public-goods-like environments), enabling punishment (with sufficient intensity) can increase group efficiency and achieve the social optimum, while enabling reward alone cannot fully eliminate inefficiency due to persistent defectors. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is a prisoner's dilemma with resource feedback, and where punishment or reward is implemented as a direct modification of payoffs. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment should be expected to increase efficiency more than enabling reward, especially when the goal is to reach the maximum possible group payoff. However, the model is theoretical and does not address practical implementation details or empirical constraints.

- source: Liu, S. Y., Zhang, C. Y., Li, K., & Zhang, J. L. (2022). Exploring the inducement for social dilemma and cooperation promotion mechanisms in structured populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that in structured populations playing iterated prisoner's dilemma with memory-one strategies, increased randomness and heterogeneity in network topology (e.g., moving from regular lattice to scale-free networks) lead to a higher prevalence of defectors and lower collective average payoff (social welfare). The key behavioral parameter is p4, the probability of cooperating after mutual defection; low p4 is associated with 'dangerous' strategies that dominate in more random/heterogeneous networks and drive payoffs toward the minimum. The authors introduce two mechanisms: (1) enforcement, which forbids p4 < 0.3, and (2) punishment, which imposes a fine on agents with p4 < 0.3. Both mechanisms, especially with sufficient punishment intensity, eliminate dangerous strategies and raise the collective average payoff substantially, regardless of network structure. The effect is robust across random regular, ER random, and BA scale-free networks.
  prediction_guidance: Although the paper studies the iterated prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, the structure is closely related, and the main outcome—collective average payoff as a fraction of the maximum possible—is directly analogous to efficiency. The findings provide strong evidence that introducing punishment (with sufficient intensity) in a repeated social dilemma robustly increases group efficiency, especially in environments where network structure would otherwise favor defection. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment targets low willingness to cooperate after mutual defection. The paper also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its strength: weak punishment may not eliminate low-cooperation strategies, but moderate to strong punishment does. These insights should inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated, networked social dilemmas, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to network-induced defection.

- source: Uchida, S., Sasaki, T., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2024). Evolution of Pay-It-Forward in the Presence of the Temptation to Free-Ride. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a donation game with institutional punishment, the evolution of cooperation and pay-it-forward strategies is strongly affected by the cognitive model of the players. Under EUT, only strong punishment (high fine or high probability) can sustain cooperation, and pay-it-forward strategies are not stable. Under PT, even weak or infrequent punishment is sufficient to deter free-riding, because players overweight small probabilities of being punished. This leads to the emergence and stability of pay-it-forward strategies and higher average payoffs (efficiency) in the population. The model shows bi-stability, with both cooperative and non-cooperative equilibria possible depending on parameters and initial conditions, but overall, punishment increases efficiency more under PT than under EUT.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games (here, a donation game) will increase group efficiency, especially when players are subject to prospect-theoretic biases. The effect is more pronounced when punishment is mild or infrequent, as real human players may overweight the risk of being punished. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the payoff-based outcomes are directly analyzed. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (even with low probability or low severity) should be expected to increase efficiency, particularly if the control game has low efficiency due to free-riding. The model also highlights the importance of psychological factors (probability weighting) in moderating the effect of punishment.

- source: Wang, S. X., Cao, M., & Chen, X. J. (2025). Optimally Combined Incentive for Cooperation Among Interacting Agents in Population Games. *Ieee Transactions on Automatic Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the optimal protocol for combining reward and punishment to promote cooperation is time-invariant and depends on the cost-to-benefit ratio and the initial and target cooperation levels. When the initial cooperation level is high, pure punishment is most cost-effective; when it is low, pure reward is more efficient; and for intermediate cases, a specific mix of reward and punishment minimizes the total cost. The minimal cost protocol is analytically derived for both well-mixed (complete graph) and regular network populations. The results are robust across different network structures. The findings are directly relevant to predicting the efficiency of institutional punishment and/or reward interventions in repeated social dilemma settings, though the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma rather than the public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of punishment (and reward) interventions in repeated social dilemma games, especially when the outcome of interest is the cost required to achieve a target level of cooperation. While the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma rather than the public goods game, the structure and logic of institutional incentives are closely related. The results suggest that the efficiency of punishment depends critically on the initial cooperation level, the cost-to-benefit ratio, and the network structure. The paper does not report group payoff or surplus in the standard PGG sense, but its focus on cumulative cost as a measure of efficiency is highly relevant for predicting the cost-effectiveness of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Zhang, Y., Hao, Q. Y., Qian, J. L., Wu, C. Y., & Bi, Y. (2024). The influence of the heterogeneities of social institutions and individuals' tendency to establish social institutions on cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous social institutions (pool punishment, anti-social punishment, and social security/reward) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma framework can promote the evolution of pro-social groups and suppress second-order free-riding. The model shows that increasing the cost of institutional investment and the growth factor of the institutional pool generally promotes cooperation and higher average payoffs, but only up to an optimal point. The introduction of a non-wasteful model with an upper limit on individual punishment/reward further improves the efficiency of the system by reducing resource waste and allowing pro-social groups to survive under harsher social dilemmas. The network (spatial) structure enhances these effects compared to well-mixed populations. While the main outcomes are strategy proportions, the paper also analyzes average payoffs, showing that institutional punishment and reward mechanisms can increase group welfare compared to baseline scenarios without such institutions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the design of punishment and reward institutions—including their cost, effectiveness, and resource allocation rules—can significantly affect the efficiency (average payoff) of groups in social dilemma games. While the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the mechanisms are closely related, especially for pool punishment and institutional reward. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (and reward) institutions, especially with non-wasteful resource allocation and optimal parameter settings, will generally increase group efficiency compared to control conditions without such institutions. The results also highlight the importance of network structure, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the presence of anti-social punishment as moderators. However, the lack of direct efficiency ratios and the use of a prisoner's dilemma rather than a PGG limit the direct quantitative transferability to PGG-like environments.

- source: Ntemos, K., Pikramenos, G., & Kalouptsidis, N. (2022). Dynamic Information Sharing and Punishment Strategies. *Ieee Transactions on Automatic Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in dynamic information sharing games with two agents, cooperation (information sharing) is not sustainable in finite-horizon settings, but can be sustained in infinite-horizon settings using punishment strategies (CGT). The equilibrium region where cooperation is optimal is characterized, and its size increases as the cost of sharing decreases or the discount factor increases. Theoretical results show that the maximal equilibrium region is unique and can be computed iteratively. Simulations confirm that lower costs and more informative observations lead to larger cooperation regions. Thus, enabling punishment strategies increases the efficiency of the system (total expected information gain minus cost) compared to the baseline where no sharing occurs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment strategies in repeated/dynamic information sharing games can increase efficiency (total payoff) compared to the baseline with no punishment, provided the game is sufficiently long (infinite horizon) and the cost of sharing is not too high. The effect is more positive when the cost of sharing is low and the discount factor is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games: the 'public good' is information, and the payoff is information gain minus cost. The results suggest that, in similar dynamic games, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, especially as the game becomes more repeated and the cost of cooperation decreases. However, the model is limited to two players, binary actions, and does not include monetary payoffs or group sizes greater than two.

- source: Hu, B., & Du, Y. X. (2023). Integrated analysis of employee cooperation and conflict behaviors in the context of digital technology. *Simulation-transactions of the Society for Modeling and Simulation International*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of punishment (penalty for conflict) is a key determinant of stable cooperation in a group setting modeled as an evolutionary game. If the penalty for conflict exceeds half the cost of cooperation, cooperation becomes the evolutionary stable strategy, and the group achieves higher average perceived payoffs. If the penalty is lower, the group exhibits unstable, turbulent behavior with frequent reversals between cooperation and conflict, and the average perceived payoff from cooperation is often lower than from conflict. The model also shows that increasing the proportion of cooperators and the perceived benefit of cooperation stabilizes group behavior. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and simulation, not on real-world or laboratory data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that increasing the penalty for non-cooperation (punishment) above a critical threshold (half the cost of cooperation) can shift group behavior from unstable, low-payoff states to stable, high-cooperation, higher-payoff states. For prediction tasks, the presence and magnitude of punishment cost and effectiveness are critical moderators. However, the outcomes are modeled as perceived payoffs and behavioral states, not as direct group efficiency ratios. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is a salient, well-understood, and enforceable mechanism, and less so for empirical prediction of actual group efficiency in experimental PGGs.

- source: Shen, C., Song, Z., Shi, L., Tanimoto, J., & Wang, Z. (2025). Exit options sustain altruistic punishment and decrease the second-order free-riders, but it is not a panacea. *Science China-information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding an exit option to a prisoner's dilemma with altruistic punishment changes the evolutionary dynamics. In well-mixed populations, exiters replace defectors as the exit payoff increases, but altruistic punishers do not become dominant. In networked populations, exiters can support the coexistence of altruistic punishers, defectors, and exiters via cyclic dominance, but this effect is unstable and depends on the exit payoff. Sometimes, non-punishing cooperators replace altruistic punishers, and the system can exhibit bi-stability between these outcomes. The effect of exiters on cooperation and punishment is nuanced and context-dependent. The paper does not provide direct predictions or measurements of group efficiency or welfare, but the results suggest that exit options can sometimes support, but also sometimes undermine, the mechanisms that sustain cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the presence and incentive level of an exit option (voluntary participation) can affect the evolutionary stability of punishment and cooperation in social dilemmas. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, it suggests that exit options can destabilize defection and, under some conditions in networked populations, support the coexistence of altruistic punishment. However, the effect is highly sensitive to the exit payoff and population structure, and exiters can also undermine punishment by enabling non-punishing cooperators to dominate. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of an exit option and its incentive level are important moderators of punishment's effect, especially in structured populations, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, L. Y., Zhao, X. B., Xie, D., & Xiao, X. (2024). On difference between direct-response method and strategy method in decision-making: behavioural and neural evidence in a reward-punishment game. *Journal of the Operational Research Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that the elicitation method (direct-response vs. strategy) significantly affects the rates of reward and punishment in a repeated two-player reward-punishment game. Specifically, the strategy method leads to higher rates of both reward and punishment. Behavioral modeling and neural evidence suggest that the ownership effect (overweighting of cost) suppresses reward in the direct-response method, while counterfactual thinking (comparison of possible outcomes) increases punishment in the strategy method. Strategic thinking is present in both methods but does not explain the difference between them. The study does not report efficiency or total payoff outcomes, and the game structure is not a public goods game or a direct variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the method of eliciting punishment and reward decisions (direct-response vs. strategy) can substantially affect the frequency of punishment and reward, with the strategy method increasing both. However, it does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The game is a two-player repeated reward-punishment game, not a public goods game or close variant, and the main outcomes are behavioral rates, not payoffs. The findings may be relevant for understanding how elicitation methods could moderate punishment effects, but the lack of efficiency data and the different game structure limit its direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Zu, J. J., Xu, F. X., Jin, T., & Xiang, W. (2022). Reward and Punishment Mechanism with weighting enhances cooperation in evolutionary games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that the introduction of a reward and punishment mechanism with weighting (RPMW) increases the frequency of cooperation in evolutionary games (PDG, SDG, SHG), both in well-mixed and lattice populations. The effect is stronger with higher intensity (alpha) and a higher proportion of supervisors (beta). The combination of reward and punishment is more effective than either alone in promoting cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment (and reward) mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games, and that the effect is stronger with higher punishment/reward intensity and more supervisors. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The specific game structure is not a public goods game but rather two-player matrix games (PDG, SDG, SHG), so transfer to PGG-like environments should be cautious.

- source: Yang, Q. X., & Yang, Y. L. (2025). Effects of an update mechanism based on combinatorial memory and high-reputation learning objects on the evolution of. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of a social monitoring mechanism (SMM) that rewards judges based on the observed cooperation rate among participants leads to higher levels of cooperation and lower corruption in the simulated two-layer network. The effect is robust across different parameter settings, including reward/fine amounts, the social reward criterion, the presence of zealots, and the strength of the reward factor. SMM is more effective than simply increasing rewards/fines in the absence of SMM. The model is applied to both Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift Game variants, showing similar qualitative results. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation/corruption rates).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which reward third-party punishers based on group cooperation rates can increase cooperation and reduce corruption in environments structurally similar to the Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift Game. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be interpreted as informative about behavioral mechanisms rather than direct efficiency effects. The model is not a standard public goods game, but the two-layer structure and the focus on punishment/reward mechanisms are adjacent to PGG-like environments. The findings suggest that adding a social monitoring/reward mechanism for punishers may increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, Y., & He, M. F. (2022). Impacts of special cooperation strategy with reward and punishment mechanism on cooperation evolution. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing special cooperators who can both reward and punish leads to a variety of stable states, including coexistence of all three strategies or pairs of strategies. Punishment is necessary to suppress defectors and enable cooperation, but excessive punishment can reduce the prevalence of special cooperators. Rewards only promote ordinary cooperation when punishment is at least moderate. The effects of reward and punishment on cooperation are non-linear: moderate levels are most effective, while too much of either can be counterproductive. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are limited to behavioral strategy frequencies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence that punishment (especially when combined with reward) can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial evolutionary games. However, because the outcomes are proportions of strategy types rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The findings suggest that moderate punishment is most effective for promoting cooperation, and that the effect of reward depends on the level of punishment. For prediction of efficiency, this paper suggests that the relationship between punishment and efficiency may be non-monotonic, with moderate punishment being optimal, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Sun, K. T., Liu, Y., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Evolution of trust in a hierarchical population with punishing investors. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that exogenously imposed collective punishment in a binary trust game significantly increases trustworthiness (from 38.5% to 82.3%) and somewhat increases trust (from 28.1% to 39.6%). When the adoption of the punishment mechanism is determined by majority vote, most subjects vote against it, and the endogenous adoption does not further increase trust or trustworthiness compared to exogenous imposition. Cognitive ability predicts voting for punishment. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes, and the main focus is on behavioral measures (trust, trustworthiness, voting).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that collective punishment can increase trustworthiness and trust in a binary trust game, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it is a binary trust game rather than a multi-player PGG. The punishment mechanism is collective and endogenous in some treatments, but the main outcomes are behavioral. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGG-like environments, this paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can improve cooperative behavior, but does not allow direct inference about efficiency effects. The absence of efficiency or payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Lee, H. W., Cleveland, C., & Szolnoki, A. (2024). Suppressing defection by increasing temptation: The impact of smart cooperators on a social dilemma situation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that adding a 'smart cooperator' strategy (who both punishes defectors and rewards cooperators, at a cost) to a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and smart cooperators. This cyclic dominance produces counter-intuitive effects: for example, increasing the temptation to defect can reduce the prevalence of defectors by indirectly supporting smart cooperators. Similarly, increasing punishment does not always reduce defection, and lowering the cost of smart cooperation can benefit unconditional cooperators more than smart cooperators. The main reported outcomes are the stationary fractions of each strategy, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how punishment and reward mechanisms interact in a spatial social dilemma with multiple strategies, but does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that the introduction of complex sanctioning strategies can lead to non-linear and sometimes counter-intuitive effects on cooperation and defection rates, but the lack of efficiency or payoff reporting limits its direct utility for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. The main value is in highlighting the potential for cyclic dominance and unexpected behavioral dynamics when multiple sanctioning strategies are present.

- source: Zhao, S. S., Pan, Q. H., Zhu, W. Q., & He, M. F. (2023). How punishing evil and promoting good promotes cooperation in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of a 'punishing evil and promoting good' strategy can promote the survival of cooperation in social dilemmas. Increasing punishment strength alone can induce the emergence of this strategy and ordinary cooperators, but is not always sufficient to eliminate defectors. Increasing reward strength can shift the system from full defection to full cooperation when the temptation to defect is low, but is insufficient when temptation is high. The most robust promotion of cooperation occurs when both punishment and reward are present, with certain combinations of minimum values able to eliminate defectors entirely. The results are presented as equilibrium proportions of each strategy, not as efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that both punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemma settings, and that their effects are complementary. However, because the outcomes are reported as equilibrium strategy frequencies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it is a prisoner's dilemma variant), and the findings suggest that the presence and strength of punishment and reward mechanisms are important moderators of cooperation, but do not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency.

- source: Wu, Y. E., Li, Z. Q., Liu, Z. Y., Cui, B. W., & Yin, A. R. (2024). Third party interventions promote cooperation on the interdependent networks: A perspective based on prospect theory. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of third-party intervention (punishment and/or reward) in a networked PD/SD game increases the fraction of cooperators in both the intervention and dispute layers. The effect is stronger when the intervention cost is lower and the intervention intensity (punishment/reward coefficients) is higher. The results are robust across different network topologies (square lattice and ER random networks) and game types (PD and SD). However, the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare; all results are in terms of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that third-party punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas, and that the effect is moderated by the cost and intensity of intervention. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. The model is also based on the PD/SD rather than a standard PGG, and is simulation/theory-based rather than empirical.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Li, L., Shang, J., & Zhao, H. (2024). Periodic update rule with Q-learning promotes evolution of cooperation in transition with mechanism. *Neurocomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing a punishment mechanism, where cooperators can choose to become punishers at a cost, increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is strongest when the punishment cost is low and the update period for strategy revision is short. The Q-learning-based periodic update rule (switching between Fermi and aspiration-driven rules) further enhances cooperation. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is not a public goods game but a spatial pairwise prisoner's dilemma with punishment and learning dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas, especially when punishment is not too costly and agents update strategies frequently. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it should be used only as weak, adjacent evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The findings suggest that low punishment cost and frequent update opportunities are likely to make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation, but the translation to efficiency is uncertain.

- source: Gao, L., Li, Y. T., Wang, Z., & Wang, R. W. (2022). Asymmetric strategy setup solve the Prisoner's Dilemma of the evolution of mutualism. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing asymmetric carrot-stick strategies (where one population can reward or punish the other) leads to a rich set of evolutionary outcomes, including stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, bistability, and dominance, depending on parameter values. The presence and strength of punishment and reward (relative to benefit) are key moderators. The model suggests that asymmetric sanctions can help maintain cooperation and avoid the tragedy of the commons, especially when the cost of cooperation is high. However, the analysis is entirely theoretical and focuses on strategy frequencies and stability, not on group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the availability and strength of punishment and reward mechanisms (especially when asymmetric) can qualitatively change the evolutionary dynamics of cooperation. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment (and reward) can stabilize cooperation under certain parameter regimes. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the presence and magnitude of punishment and reward (relative to benefit and cost) are important moderators, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment/reward can sustain cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Guo, T., Du, C. P., & Shi, L. (2024). Evolution of cooperation on interdependent networks: The impact of asymmetric punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that asymmetric punishment, where only one group can punish another and only across network layers, can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in spatial prisoner's dilemma games on interdependent networks. The effect is stronger when the punishment is more severe (higher fine per unit cost). When the temptation to defect is low, punishment is more effective than reward at promoting cooperation; when the temptation is high, reward becomes more effective. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The paper does not report efficiency or total group payoff, so its findings are most directly relevant to predicting cooperation rates rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in agent-based models of spatial prisoner's dilemma games with asymmetric punishment, increasing punishment strength or effectiveness increases cooperation rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling or strengthening punishment will likely increase cooperation rates, which may be correlated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly tested. The paper also shows that the effect of punishment depends on the temptation to defect and the cost-to-fine ratio. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding how punishment design (asymmetry, cost-to-fine ratio) affects cooperation rates, with only indirect implications for efficiency.

- source: Wen, Y. J., He, Z. X., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Indirect punishment can outperform direct punishment in promoting cooperation in structured populations. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, in structured populations modeled as a square lattice, indirect punishment (punishing defectors who harm one's neighbors) can outperform direct punishment (punishing one's own defectors) in promoting cooperation, but only when both the cost and fine of punishment are low. Outside this narrow parameter region, direct punishment may be more effective, both may be equally effective, or neither may improve cooperation. The mechanism is that indirect punishment can impose more total fines on defectors without greatly increasing the cost to punishers, but only when punishment is cheap. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies; group efficiency or total payoff is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the cost and scope of punishment affect cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas.
