  findings: The introduction of an optional external supervision service can reduce corruption and promote cooperation, but its effectiveness depends on the cost of supervision and the mutation rates of players and enforcers. Lowering the cost of supervision is not always optimal: if the cost is too low, unnecessary supervision may occur, while if it is too high, corruption may persist. The initial fraction of honest enforcers and the relative mutation rates are critical: higher mutation rates for enforcers (relative to players) favor honest enforcement and trusting cooperation. In finite populations, the system may reach stable equilibria or exhibit persistent oscillations in the fractions of cooperators, defectors, and corrupt enforcers. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence about how punishment, corruption, and external supervision interact in a social dilemma context with bribery and enforcement. It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff as a function of game design dimensions. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (via external supervision) is highly sensitive to the cost of supervision and the mutation rates of enforcers and players. The model is adjacent to public goods games, but is not a standard PGG, and the main outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions), not efficiency. Thus, the paper is more useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators of punishment and corruption than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Wang, W. J., Xu, Z. H., Hua, S. J., Cui, L. Q., Zhang, J. L., & Meng, F. Y. (2024). Threshold-initiated spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that intergroup competition significantly increases the rate of volunteering in the volunteer's dilemma, even when there is no additional monetary incentive or change in the payoff structure. Risk (a 50% chance of public good failure) reduces volunteering rates, but intergroup competition mitigates this negative effect. Gender and risk aversion moderate these effects: females volunteer more in no-risk settings, males increase volunteering more under risky intergroup competition. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but notes in the discussion that over-volunteering (stimulated by intergroup competition) is socially inefficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are about volunteering rates in a volunteer's dilemma, which is structurally adjacent to but not the same as a standard public goods game. The results suggest that intergroup competition increases pro-social action rates, especially under risk, but also that this can lead to inefficiency through over-volunteering. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the main takeaway is that mechanisms increasing pro-social action (like competition) may not always improve efficiency, especially in anti-coordination settings like the volunteer's dilemma. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the paper's predictive value for the target task.

- source: Yuan, H. R., Meng, X. Z., & Alzahrani, A. K. (2023). Analysis of dynamic evolution process of the N-player division of labor game model. *Mathematical Modelling of Natural Phenomena*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that high toxicity rates (especially against cheaters), high toxin levels, and smaller group sizes promote the disappearance of cheaters and the persistence of cooperation (cooperators and/or toxin producers). The system exhibits bistability: depending on initial conditions and parameters, the population may converge to all cooperators, coexistence of cooperators and toxin producers, or coexistence of cooperators and cheaters. The interior equilibrium (all three strategies coexisting) is always unstable. Bifurcation analysis reveals thresholds in absorption rates and toxin parameters that determine which strategies persist. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how costly sanctioning (here, toxin production) can suppress free riding and promote cooperation in N-player games. However, the model is not a standard public goods game with explicit peer punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that increasing the effectiveness or prevalence of costly sanctions (analogous to punishment) can reduce free riding and increase the fraction of task-performers, especially in smaller groups and with higher sanction effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes or direct mapping to standard PGG dimensions.

- source: Bruner, J. P., & Smead, R. (2022). Tag-based spite with correlated interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of observable tags and the possibility of conditioning behavior on tags (e.g., only harming out-group members) fundamentally changes the evolutionary dynamics of costly harming (spite) in the Prisoner's Delight game. Tags make pure (unconditional) spite less likely to evolve even when anti-correlation would otherwise favor it, but make conditional (tag-based) spite more likely in some regimes. Several stable equilibria are identified, including cooperative polymorphisms (mostly cooperation, little spite), pure spite, and a novel 'treacherous polymorphism' where spite is directed within groups. The presence of tags generally reduces the prevalence of widespread spiteful behavior, even under conditions that would otherwise favor it. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and behavioral frequencies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The model is a two-player game (Prisoner's Delight) with costly harming, not a multi-player public goods game. The main outcomes are the evolutionary frequencies of behavioral types, not group efficiency or total payoff. However, the analysis is relevant for understanding how the structure of punishment-like mechanisms (costly harming, conditional on observable tags) and population structure (correlated/anti-correlated interactions) can affect the prevalence of cooperation and spite. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing for conditional punishment (e.g., based on group identity) can both promote and suppress harmful behavior, depending on the context. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect theoretical context about how punishment-like behaviors may interact with population structure and signaling, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Hu, Q., Jin, T., Jiang, Y. L., & Liu, X. W. (2024). Reputation incentives with public supervision promote cooperation in evolutionary games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that a reputation incentive mechanism with public supervision can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG. The effect is stronger when the evaluation intensity (the range of possible evaluation values) is lower, allowing players to more quickly update their evaluations and reputations. The model demonstrates that reputation-based rewards can compensate cooperators for losses due to defectors, promoting the formation and persistence of cooperative clusters. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based reward mechanisms with public supervision can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG-like environments, especially when evaluation intensity is low. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of reputation and reward mechanisms as adjacent or complementary to punishment, but should not be used as direct evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Li, G. Y., Du, H. F., & He, X. C. (2024). The evolutionary prisoner's dilemma game in continuous signed networks. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that negative relationships, modeled as punitive ties that reduce payoffs for defectors, can promote the emergence and stability of cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is stronger when the negative coefficient (punishment strength) or the proportion of negative ties is higher. Adaptive networks, where agents can adjust their ties, further facilitate cooperation. Structural balance (alignment of positive ties within groups and negative ties between groups) also supports higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, focusing instead on the behavioral outcome of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, negative relationships that reduce payoffs) can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is the proportion of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, and because the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The findings suggest that increasing the cost or prevalence of punishment can promote cooperation, but do not quantify the effect on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Huang, S. X., Liu, X. S., Hu, Y. H., & Fu, X. (2023). The influence of confidence in aspiration-fulfillment strategy on prisoner's dilemma evolution. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that a confidence mechanism—where confidence in a strategy is updated based on the difference between payoff and aspiration—can promote and stabilize cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect of the confidence increment parameter (alpha) is non-monotonic: smaller alpha can lead to higher cooperation under some conditions, but too small an alpha or high temptation to defect undermines cooperation. Common aspirations in the group support higher cooperation than heterogeneous aspirations. There is a threshold effect for aspiration: below the threshold, increasing aspiration increases cooperation, but above it, cooperation collapses. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on confidence and aspiration mechanisms in spatial prisoner's dilemma games may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how psychological or updating mechanisms affect cooperation, but not for the efficiency prediction task as defined. The absence of payoff or efficiency outcomes and the lack of punishment or reward mechanisms mean this paper should not be weighted for the core prediction task.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., Yang, S., Chen, X. J., Bai, Y. B., & Xie, G. M. (2023). Reputation update of responders efficiently promotes the evolution of fairness in the ultimatum game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect reciprocity mechanisms based on reputation can strongly promote fairness in the ultimatum game. Specifically, updating responders' reputation via image scoring leads to a very high level of fairness (over 95%), while updating proposers' reputation alone increases fairness above the baseline (no reputation) for many social norms, with a few norms achieving over 80% fairness. When both proposers' and responders' reputations are updated, most social norms yield even higher fairness than updating proposers' reputation alone, but never exceed the effect of updating responders' reputation alone. The results are based on evolutionary game-theoretic modeling and analysis of strategy dynamics, not on empirical or experimental data. The main dependent variable is the frequency of fair offers and rejections of unfair offers, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly, it analyzes how indirect reciprocity and reputation mechanisms affect fairness (a behavioral outcome) in the ultimatum game, which is a related but distinct environment. The findings suggest that mechanisms analogous to punishment (here, rejection of unfair offers, i.e., altruistic punishment) can strongly promote norm compliance and fairness, especially when reputation is at stake. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game, the results should be used only as weak, indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wang, J., Tian, J. C., & Li, X. P. (2024). Enhancement of cooperation induced by information-payoff evolution on two-layer complex networks. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that introducing dynamic elimination (removal of defectors) in a networked donation game greatly increases cooperation frequency, but at the cost of eliminating many players. Adding the ZD strategy further increases cooperation and allows more players to remain in the game. The combination of elimination and ZD strategy is robust to changes in game parameters and identification errors. Larger-degree nodes are more likely to survive, and the optimal initial strategy for survival depends on whether ZD is present. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on behavioral and structural outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms analogous to punishment (elimination of defectors) and sophisticated strategies (ZD) can increase cooperation and player retention in dynamic networked games. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral and structural consequences of punishment-like mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Khatun, K., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Stability of pairwise social dilemma games: Destructive agents, constructive agents, and their joint effects. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in pairwise social dilemma games, introducing destructive agents (who opt out and harm others) does not promote cooperation, in contrast to their effect in public goods games. Instead, destructive agents destabilize or replace defection (in prisoner's dilemma and stag-hunt) or undermine cooperation (in harmony and chicken games). Constructive agents (who opt out and benefit others) can have similar destabilizing effects if their own payoff is high, but if their payoff is lower than what they give, they mainly destabilize defection without harming cooperation. When both agent types are present, the system exhibits complex multistable equilibria, but cooperation is not promoted. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not model punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes, and its focus is on pairwise (dyadic) games rather than multiplayer public goods games. The closest relevance is in its discussion of how certain opt-out strategies (destructive/constructive agents) affect the stability of cooperation, but not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted strongly in predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding the limits of voluntary participation and opt-out mechanisms in pairwise games, but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Zhang, Q. W., Liu, J. Q., & Zhang, X. R. (2024). Reputation-based disconnection-reconnection mechanism in Prisoner's Dilemma Game within dynamic networks. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that a reputation-based mechanism, where players can disconnect from low-reputation neighbors and reconnect with others, significantly increases the frequency of cooperation in repeated PDG simulations on dynamic networks. The effect is stronger when the reputation factor (alpha) is high and when the network's information connectivity (environmental degree r, rewiring probability p) is high. Conversely, when the selection intensity (sigma) is high, the effect of reputation is diminished and cooperation collapses. The results are consistent across both square lattice and small-world network topologies. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly, it shows that reputation-based dynamic linking mechanisms can strongly increase cooperation rates in repeated PDG environments. The findings suggest that, in analogous PGG-like settings, mechanisms that allow for social selection or partner choice based on reputation may increase cooperation, which could plausibly translate into higher efficiency if payoffs are aligned with cooperation. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency changes. The results are most relevant for settings where dynamic partner selection or reputation-based exclusion is possible.

- source: Li, S. L., He, Z. X., Jia, D. Y., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Granting leaders priority exit options promotes and jeopardizes cooperation in social dilemmas. *Neurocomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that granting exit rights to high-degree (leader) nodes in a spatial prisoner's dilemma can either suppress or promote cooperation, depending on the strength of the social dilemma and the network's assortativity. In weak dilemmas, leader-priority exit reduces cooperation, while in strong dilemmas, it can promote cooperation by isolating defectors and allowing cooperative clusters to survive among low-degree nodes. Random and fringe-priority exit have weaker or negligible effects. The main outcome is the relative cooperation ratio among non-exiters. The model does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism per se, but can act as a hard sanction by denying payoffs to others.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma with an exit option, not a PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism, though it can function as a hard sanction. The findings may be relevant for understanding how voluntary exclusion or exit options affect cooperation in networked dilemmas, but do not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. Use this paper only for very indirect, mechanistic insights about how exclusion or exit options might interact with network structure and dilemma strength.

- source: Lu, S. N., & Wang, Y. (2024). Adjustment of link weights based on tolerance promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reduce interaction intensity with neighbors who defect beyond a tolerance threshold increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. However, if the tolerance threshold is too high (i.e., individuals are too forgiving), cooperation is undermined. The effect is stronger when more individuals in the population are 'environmental adaptors' who adjust their interaction intensity. The main outcome is the equilibrium frequency of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings are robust to changes in network size and initial cooperator fraction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about mechanisms that may promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, specifically through adaptive partner selection or ostracism based on tolerance for defection. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The results suggest that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce interaction with defectors (after a tolerance threshold) can increase cooperation rates, but excessive tolerance undermines this effect. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not analyze punishment costs, efficiency, or public goods structures.

- source: Lu, S. N., & Wang, Y. (2025). Experience-driven learning and interactive rules under link weight adjustment promote cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that incorporating both direct and indirect experience in interaction and strategy update rules, as well as introducing tolerance in link weight adjustment, significantly promotes cooperation in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma simulations. High sensitivity to defection (low tolerance) and greater weight on indirect experience both increase the frequency of cooperation and the average payoff of cooperators relative to defectors. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare outcomes. The findings are based on simulation results and are not directly transferable to public goods games with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms allowing individuals to avoid defectors (via adaptive link weights and social learning) can increase cooperation and the average payoff of cooperators in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma settings. However, it does not study public goods games, does not implement explicit punishment or sanctioning, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare. Thus, its relevance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is limited and should be considered only as weak, mechanistic context.

- source: Zhang, Y., Hao, Q. Y., Qian, J. L., Wu, C. Y., Guo, N., & Ling, X. (2024). The cooperative evolution in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game with the local loyalty of two-strategy. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that incorporating local loyalty to both cooperation and defection strategies in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can promote the emergence and stability of cooperation, especially when the strength index of local loyalty is positive and the weighting between payoff and loyalty is balanced. There are optimal parameter values that maximize cooperation frequency, and the system can reach full cooperation under these conditions. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and all results are based on simulation of behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates and loyalty patterns).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. While it explores mechanisms (loyalty, reputation) that are conceptually adjacent to punishment and cooperation, it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings may be contextually informative for understanding how social mechanisms can promote cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence for the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Feng, S. N., & Liu, X. S. (2024). Effects of three-faced strategy on the evolution of cooperation in social dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of a 'three-faced' strategy—one that defects against cooperators, punishes defectors, and cooperates with its own kind—can suppress defection and promote cooperative behavior in a population, depending on parameter values. Low role-switching costs or high government rewards for punishing defectors favor the spread of the three-faced strategy and/or cooperators. High punishment costs or high temptation to defect favor defectors. The model identifies parameter regimes where cooperators, defectors, and three-faced individuals coexist, or where one strategy dominates. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary stability and proportions of each strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical evidence about the effects of punishment-like mechanisms in a prisoner's dilemma context with a novel adaptive strategy. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it suggests that punishment-like behaviors (when not too costly and/or when subsidized) can suppress defection and support the maintenance of cooperation, especially when adaptive strategies are possible. The findings may inform qualitative expectations about the role of punishment cost, effectiveness, and incentives, but do not provide quantitative efficiency data for prediction.

- source: Lu, Z. L., Xing, L., Xu, R. X., Li, M. S., & Li, J. M. (2024). Stochastic evolution game analysis of the strategic coalition of enterprise pollution control. *Aims Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the probability of enterprises joining a pollution control coalition depends on the relative costs and benefits, the sharing of profits, and the initial willingness to cooperate. Under certain parameter conditions, all enterprises will join the coalition (full cooperation), while under others, none will join. The model shows that the presence of random disturbances (stochasticity) leads to fluctuations in strategies but does not fundamentally alter the stable outcomes. The analysis highlights the importance of effective incentive and punishment mechanisms for coalition stability, but these are discussed at a conceptual level rather than modeled as explicit game interventions. The main findings are about the conditions for stable coalition formation, not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides only indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. While it discusses the importance of incentive and punishment mechanisms for coalition stability, it does not model or experimentally test the effect of enabling or disabling punishment on group efficiency or payoff. The model is adjacent to PGGs in that it studies cooperation among multiple agents with shared benefits and costs, but the main outcomes are behavioral (coalition formation) rather than efficiency. The paper may inform mechanistic expectations about the role of incentives and punishment in sustaining cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Gong, H. W., Xiang, H. J., Wang, Y. F., Gao, H. J., & Meng, X. Z. (2024). Strategy evolution of a novel cooperative game model induced by reward feedback and a time delay. *Aims Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game with dynamic, feedback-based rewards (but no punishment), the system can exhibit multiple stable equilibria, bistability, and oscillatory dynamics depending on parameters and time delay. Increasing the reward does not always increase cooperation; under some conditions, even maximal rewards do not induce cooperation. The initial state can determine which equilibrium is reached. Introducing time delay can destabilize previously stable equilibria and induce stable limit cycles (oscillations) in the proportion of cooperators and reward intensity. The model does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is a theoretical analysis of a different class of evolutionary games (general 2x2 matrix) with dynamic rewards and time delay, focusing on the evolution of cooperation and reward intensity. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and no implementation of punishment or sanctions. The findings may be of limited, indirect interest for understanding how dynamic incentives and time delays affect cooperation dynamics, but they do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A. (2023). Inequality leads to the evolution of intolerance in reputation-based populations. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that economic inequality is a strong enhancer of intolerance in reputation-based populations. Intolerant strategies can invade tolerant groups more easily when inequality is high, even if overall economic stress is moderate. Redistribution of wealth can prevent or reverse the spread of intolerance, but only if implemented before intolerance becomes widespread. The model shows that tolerant minorities under economic stress may reduce their willingness to punish defectors or may even help wealthy discriminators. The main reported outcomes are the intention to cooperate and the evolutionary stability of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that in reputation-based cooperation environments, inequality and group structure can strongly moderate the effectiveness of punishment-like mechanisms (here, reputation-based exclusion) in sustaining cooperation. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings are most useful for understanding how inequality and group structure might interact with punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Pandula, N., Akcay, E., & Morsky, B. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with abductive reasoning. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that abductive reasoning in indirect reciprocity models can, under certain social norms and low error rates, promote higher levels of cooperation than non-reasoning models. For the Scoring norm, abductive reasoning enables stable cooperative states that are not possible without reasoning. For other norms (Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), abductive reasoning can outperform or underperform non-reasoning depending on the error rates and the degree of confirmation/falsification bias. Under the Shunning norm, abductive reasoning leads to only defection. The model is more robust to errors in observation/assessment than to errors in action. The main reported outcomes are the equilibrium frequencies of cooperative strategies and intended cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how reasoning about errors and reputation assignment can affect the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it suggests that the cognitive process for assigning reputations (abductive reasoning) and the social norm in use can be critical moderators of cooperation rates.   findings: The study finds that tie retention (the ability to keep social ties with ex-neighbors after migration) can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game, even when migration is random. The maximum number of retained ex-neighbors (m) is a key parameter: higher m leads to higher cooperation rates. Social diversity in tie retention (variation in m across players) further enhances cooperation. Migration probability (p) has a non-monotonic effect: lower p generally favors cooperation, but diversity in p can create optimal conditions for cooperation. The results challenge the previous view that random migration is always detrimental to cooperation. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.

For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that social stickiness (tie retention) and migration patterns are important moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

The authors review circumstantial evidence from studies of psychopathy, social value orientation, and economic games (e.g., prisoner's dilemma, public goods) to suggest that both prosocial and antisocial (extortionate) strategies can be supported by intuitive processes. However, the paper does not provide new empirical findings or direct evidence on the effects of punishment on efficiency or payoffs in public goods or similar games.

- source: Bae, M., Shimada, T., & Baek, S. K. (2024). Exact cluster dynamics of indirect reciprocity in complete graphs. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper provides an exact theoretical analysis of how different indirect reciprocity norms (L4 and L6) shape the long-run social structure in large, fully connected populations. Under L6 (stern judging), the population almost always segregates into two mutually hostile clusters as the group size increases, due to the entropy of balanced configurations. Under L4, a subtle change in the assessment rule leads to a high probability of the 'paradise' state, where everyone assesses everyone else positively, especially as group size increases. The findings are about the stationary distribution of assessment configurations and the emergence of social structure, not about efficiency or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. While it is highly informative about the structural consequences of different indirect reciprocity norms (i.e., whether societies segregate or reach universal cooperation), it does not model or report payoffs, efficiency, or welfare. The results may be relevant for understanding the conditions under which cooperation is structurally stable, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency.

- source: Lee, H. W., & Weng, Y. N. (2025). Granular Q-learning adaptation boosts collective welfare in multi-agent Prisoner's Dilemma. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that more granular, neighbor-specific Q-learning (ID) enables agents to achieve higher collective welfare (average payoff) and maintain cooperation more robustly than aggregated Q-learning (II) in multi-agent Prisoner's Dilemma games. This advantage is most pronounced at intermediate dilemma strengths and in homogeneous networks, but persists in scale-free networks as well. The results are based on extensive simulations varying network structure, dilemma strength, and agent composition. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present; the only difference is the learning architecture. The findings suggest that the granularity of learning adaptation is a key moderator of efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial and scale-free Prisoner's Dilemma environments, the granularity of learning adaptation (neighbor-specific vs. aggregated) is a major determinant of collective efficiency. While not a public goods game or a punishment study, the results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the learning rule or information granularity. The absence of punishment/reward means the findings are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs, but the payoff-based outcome and the focus on learning architecture offer useful indirect evidence for how cognitive sophistication or information structure can boost efficiency. The results are most applicable to settings where agent adaptation, rather than explicit sanctions, is the main intervention.

- source: Das Bairagya, J., Mondal, S. S., Chowdhury, D., & Chakraborty, S. (2023). Eco-evolutionary games for harvesting self-renewing common resource: effect of growing harvester population. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.

  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the realization or prevention of the tragedy of the commons (TOC) in a common-pool resource system depends critically on the relationship between the resource's intrinsic growth rate and the harvesting rates of the population, as well as on the incentive structure encoded in the payoff matrix. When the resource's growth rate is between the low and high harvesting rates, both collapse and partial/complete prevention of TOC are possible, depending on initial conditions and parameter values. Finite population size (carrying capacity) can introduce bistability and limit cycles, making the system's fate sensitive to initial conditions. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, but suggests that such extensions could be studied in future work. The main outcome relevant to efficiency is the long-run resource state, which serves as a proxy for group welfare or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how resource dynamics, harvesting strategies, and population size interact to determine group efficiency in common-pool resource settings. While it does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, it offers a detailed analysis of how efficiency (resource state) depends on game parameters such as harvesting rates, resource growth, and population carrying capacity. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in the absence of punishment or reward, and for highlighting the importance of ecological feedbacks and population size. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does suggest that efficiency is highly sensitive to the incentive structure and ecological parameters. The discussion of future extensions to include policing or punishment indicates that the model could be adapted for such analyses.

  findings: The study finds that individual accumulated payoff is the most important predictor of cooperation/defection decisions, more so than the outcome of the previous round. Behavioral clustering improves the predictability of individual moves, with F1-scores for predicting cooperation/defection reaching up to 95% in some clusters. The diversity of human strategies is substantial, but clustering allows for more accurate modeling. The experiment does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes. The findings are most relevant for understanding individual decision-making and the role of payoff history, rather than for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.

The design is a repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with optional partner selection (refusal to play), which is only weakly analogous to punishment. The main insight is that accumulated payoff is a strong driver of individual cooperation, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency. The paper may be useful for understanding the importance of payoff history in individual decision-making, but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Garbus, J., & Pollack, J. (2024). Emergent Resource Exchange and Tolerated Theft Behavior Using Multiagent Reinforcement Learning. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that agents in a foraging environment with periodic forced congregation (via a campfire and night penalty) can learn to exchange resources, leading to higher cumulative group reward (efficiency) than agents in environments without such structure. The emergence of cooperation is sensitive to environmental parameters: with a strong night penalty, agents reliably learn to trade and achieve higher rewards; with a weak or absent penalty, agents do not reliably trade and achieve lower rewards. No explicit punishment or sanctioning is present; instead, agents develop indirect defenses against defection (e.g., exchange protocols that prevent being cheated). The study provides evidence that environmental design, rather than explicit punishment, can be sufficient for the emergence of efficient cooperation in multiagent systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or manipulated. However, it is informative for prediction tasks in that it shows efficient cooperation and high group payoff can emerge in the absence of punishment, given appropriate environmental structure (e.g., forced repeated interaction, resource interdependence, and congregation). For prediction, this suggests that the presence or absence of punishment is not the only moderator of efficiency: environmental design can be equally or more important. The paper does not inform the marginal effect of enabling punishment relative to a control, so it should not be used to estimate the effect of punishment per se.

  findings: The study shows that in a networked trust game, allowing investors to adjust their investment amounts based on local trust levels (heterogeneous investment) significantly increases total group assets and the prevalence of trust, compared to homogeneous investment. As the heterogeneity parameter alpha increases, the system transitions from collapse (no investors, zero assets) to stable coexistence of investors and trustworthy trustees, with high group efficiency. The effect is robust to initial conditions and persists even under strong social dilemmas. However, the model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for the treatment effect of punishment, but may provide indirect context for the role of contribution heterogeneity.

- source: Gros, C. (2022). Collective strategy condensation towards class-separated societies. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that adding an envy-based psychological component to agents' utility functions in a competitive resource allocation game leads to a phase transition: for low envy, agents play diverse pure and mixed strategies, but for high envy, a large fraction of agents adopt the same mixed strategy, forming a lower class. This transition is accompanied by a substantial drop in mean income (efficiency), indicating that envy is detrimental to group welfare. The results are robust to variations in model details and to heterogeneity in envy levels. The paper also estimates, using ultimatum game data, that real-world levels of envy are likely high enough to induce this class-stratified, low-efficiency regime.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that adding a psychological penalty for relative disadvantage (envy) to a competitive resource allocation game reduces group efficiency/welfare, especially beyond a critical threshold. While the game is not a public goods game or a punishment game, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where psychological or social comparison motives are present. However, the paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or public goods provision directly, so its predictive value is limited to adjacent cases where social comparison or envy-like terms are present in the utility function. The main actionable insight is that increasing the weight of social comparison (envy) reduces efficiency, even when the underlying game structure is otherwise unchanged.

- source: Bellucci, G. (2022). A Model of Trust. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper formalizes trust as a trade-off between vulnerability (potential loss) and positive expectations of a partner's behavior, using a computational model applied to the investment game. Simulations show that trustful investors (high trust attitude) invest more and are more likely to recover trust after breaches, but are also more vulnerable to exploitation. Loss aversion reduces investment and makes trust repair harder, while positive expectations and evidence of partner benevolence or competence increase investment. The model predicts that both trust attitude and learning about the partner's behavior jointly determine investment and, by extension, payoffs. The model is extended to consider both benevolence and competence of the trustee, showing that both traits affect investment and expected payoffs. However, the paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or public goods games, and does not report empirical efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is a theoretical modeling paper focused on the investment game (trust game), which is adjacent but not equivalent to the public goods game. The model and its simulations may inform understanding of how psychological factors (trust, expectations, loss aversion) affect investment and payoffs in dyadic trust settings, but do not address punishment, sanctions, or group-level efficiency in multi-player public goods contexts. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Lu, W., & Liang, S. (2025). The role of strategy-affected emotions in the evolution of cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial three-strategy prisoner's dilemma model with emotion dynamics, increasing the intensity of friendliness (the degree to which strategies affect emotions) leads to higher levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs in the population. The effect is robust across different parameterizations of the game (including donor-recipient and boundary PD variants). Memory length moderates the effect: with low friendliness, longer memory increases cooperation; with high friendliness, longer memory decreases cooperation. The model demonstrates that stable, high-payoff cooperation can emerge through emotion-strategy feedbacks alone, without any punishment or reward mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is relevant as a theoretical demonstration that high cooperation and group payoff can be achieved through emotion-based mechanisms, without punishment or reward. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper suggests that alternative mechanisms (like friendliness) can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency, but it does not inform how punishment would change efficiency relative to a control. The game design dimensions relevant to punishment (punishment_cost, punishment_tech, etc.) are not present or manipulated. Thus, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment or reward.

The outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates and the coexistence of different strategies, not in terms of efficiency or group payoff.

  findings: The study finds that certain conditional cooperation strategies, especially those tolerant of cooperative behavior and harsh toward defection (the 'catalyst strategy'), can promote high levels of cooperation in indirect reciprocity models based on second-order action labels. Larger populations enhance the effectiveness of the catalyst strategy, while smaller populations and higher noise diminish it.   findings: The study finds that introducing a community learning mechanism—where individuals preferentially imitate the most successful community members—substantially increases the proportion of cooperators in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma simulation. The emergence of large, cohesive communities is associated with high cooperation rates. However, the paper does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and there are no punishment or reward mechanisms in the model.

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game where players can adopt different strategies toward each neighbor (interactive diversity) and update their strategies based on an aspiration threshold, the fraction of cooperation (F_c) can be significantly increased by tuning the aspiration parameter (alpha). There is an optimal range of aspiration for each level of temptation to defect (b), and too high or too low aspiration can suppress cooperation. The results are presented as phase diagrams and time series of F_c, but no group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes are reported. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the model.

The game structure is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game or its direct variant.

The proportion of individuals using each pattern and the estimation level of partner ability are key moderators: moderate estimation levels maximize cooperation, while too low or too high estimation levels reduce it. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation density, not efficiency or group payoff.

The results are robust across weak and true prisoner's dilemma parameterizations. However, the paper does not study punishment, reward, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.
